# BIO qs bank — extracted MCQ source archive

- Source: `BIO qs bank.pdf` (138 pages, scanned/OCR assisted).

- The source includes written prompts and cases in addition to these MCQs; all visible written sections are transcribed as QROC items in this file. No lecture transcripts were created.

**Source Question:** p. 80, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** ATP supplies energy and transfers phosphoryl groups; ADP is the product of ATP hydrolysis and substrate for ATP regeneration; AMP is an RNA building block and an allosteric energy-status signal; cAMP is a second messenger in hormone signaling.

**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry

### Q621 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 1): On biochemical grounds, explain why dietary methylated purines are not contraindicated in hyperuricemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 1
**EXP:** Dietary methylxanthines such as caffeine and theophylline are not metabolized like the purine nucleosides of nucleic acids into a large urate load. Their methylated metabolites are handled separately, so they are not treated as equivalent to high-purine foods such as organ meats in the source context.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q622 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 2): Explain how cAMP acts as a hormone second messenger.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 2
**EXP:** A hormone binds its cell-surface receptor and activates a G protein. GTP-bound Gαs stimulates adenylyl cyclase, which converts ATP to cAMP. cAMP activates protein kinase A, leading to phosphorylation of target proteins and the cellular response. Phosphodiesterases terminate the signal by hydrolyzing cAMP.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q623 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 3): Explain the biological importance of dietary methylated purines and give two examples.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 3
**EXP:** Methylated purines have biologic and pharmacologic activity. Caffeine acts as a stimulant, while theophylline is a bronchodilator; theobromine is another example with mild diuretic and smooth-muscle effects.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q624 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 4): Explain the different effects of glucagon and insulin on cAMP.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 4
**EXP:** Glucagon activates Gs-coupled signaling in hepatocytes, stimulating adenylyl cyclase and increasing cAMP/PKA activity. Insulin generally lowers cAMP signaling by promoting cAMP breakdown and opposing glucagon-driven phosphorylation, favoring dephosphorylation and storage pathways.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q625 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 5): Explain the role of nitric oxide as a vasodilator.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 5
**EXP:** Endothelial nitric oxide synthase forms NO from L-arginine. NO diffuses into vascular smooth muscle and activates soluble guanylyl cyclase, increasing cGMP and protein kinase G activity. This lowers smooth-muscle contractile tone and causes vasodilation.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q626 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Explain 6): Explain why mitochondrial myopathies may show maternal inheritance.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Explain 6
**EXP:** Mitochondrial DNA is transmitted almost exclusively through the oocyte because paternal mitochondria are normally eliminated after fertilization. A mother can transmit mutant mtDNA to all her children, while disease severity varies with heteroplasmy and the proportion of mutant mitochondria in each tissue.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q627 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Compare 1): Compare DNA and RNA.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Compare 1
**EXP:** DNA contains deoxyribose and thymine, is usually double-stranded, and stores long-term genetic information. RNA contains ribose and uracil, is usually single-stranded, and functions in gene expression and regulation. The 2′-OH makes RNA more chemically labile than DNA.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q628 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Compare 2): Compare A-DNA and Z-DNA.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Compare 2
**EXP:** A-DNA is a right-handed helix, favored by dehydration and found in some DNA-RNA hybrids; it is shorter and wider than B-DNA. Z-DNA is a left-handed helix with a zigzag backbone, favored by alternating purine-pyrimidine sequences such as GC repeats and certain ionic or supercoiling conditions.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q629 (Nucleotides and Nucleic Acid Chemistry, written source p. 81, Compare 3): Compare monocistronic and polycistronic mRNA.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 81, Compare 3
**EXP:** Monocistronic mRNA contains one principal coding region and produces one polypeptide; it is typical of eukaryotic nuclear mRNA. Polycistronic mRNA contains multiple coding regions and can produce several polypeptides from one transcript; it is common in prokaryotic operons.
**Source Pages:** 81
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q630 (Myocardial Infarction, written source p. 122, Enumerate 1): Enumerate four biochemical markers used in myocardial infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 122, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Cardiac troponin I, cardiac troponin T, CK-MB, and myoglobin are commonly used markers. LDH, especially the LDH-1 pattern, is a classic additional marker.
**Source Pages:** 122
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q631 (Myocardial Infarction, written source p. 122, Enumerate 2): Enumerate four risk factors for myocardial infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 122, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Cigarette smoking, hypertension, dyslipidemia, and diabetes mellitus are major modifiable risk factors. Age, male sex, obesity, and family history are additional risk factors.
**Source Pages:** 122
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q632 (Myocardial Infarction, written source p. 122, Enumerate 3): Enumerate four clinical symptoms or signs of myocardial infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 122, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Prolonged central chest pressure or pain, pain radiating to the arm/jaw/back, dyspnea, and diaphoresis are common. Nausea, vomiting, weakness, and syncope may also occur.
**Source Pages:** 122
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q633 (Myocardial Infarction, written source p. 122, Explain 1): Explain why troponins are not preferred for diagnosing reinfarction or extension of myocardial infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 122, Explain 1
**EXP:** Cardiac troponins remain elevated for several days after an infarction, so a new event may not produce a clearly separable second rise while the initial level is still high. CK-MB returns toward baseline sooner and is therefore more useful for detecting a new rise.
**Source Pages:** 122
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q634 (Myocardial Infarction, written source p. 122, Explain 2): Explain why CK-MB is useful for diagnosing reinfarction or extension of infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 122, Explain 2
**EXP:** CK-MB rises after myocardial injury and usually returns toward baseline within about two to three days. A second rise after an initial decline can indicate reinfarction or extension, unlike troponin which stays elevated longer.
**Source Pages:** 122
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q635 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Enumerate 1): Enumerate the sequence of events when a class I hormone binds its intracellular receptor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** The lipophilic hormone enters the cell and binds its cytosolic or nuclear receptor. Ligand binding changes receptor conformation, promotes dimerization and DNA-binding activity, and (for a cytosolic receptor) allows nuclear translocation. The complex binds a hormone-response element, recruits co-regulators and transcription machinery, and changes gene transcription and protein synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -

**Source Question:** p. 125, Enumerate 3

**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q638 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Enumerate 4): Name hormones that mediate their actions through calcium and/or phosphoinositides.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Examples include α1-adrenergic catecholamines, angiotensin II, thyrotropin-releasing hormone, gonadotropin-releasing hormone, oxytocin, and vasopressin acting at V1 receptors. These activate Gq, phospholipase C, IP3, DAG, and intracellular Ca²⁺ signaling.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q639 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Enumerate 5): Name hormones that mediate their actions through protein kinase activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Enumerate 5
**EXP:** Insulin and insulin-like growth factors signal through receptor tyrosine kinases. Growth hormone and prolactin signal through receptor-associated JAK kinases; many other hormones activate intracellular kinase cascades downstream of second messengers.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q640 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Compare 1): Compare general features of class I and class II hormones.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Compare 1
**EXP:** Class I hormones are generally lipophilic, cross the plasma membrane, and bind intracellular receptors that regulate transcription; responses have slower onset but longer duration. Class II hormones are generally hydrophilic, bind cell-surface receptors, and signal through second messengers or kinase cascades; responses are usually faster and shorter-lived.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q641 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Explain 1): Explain the role of cAMP as a second hormone messenger.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Explain 1
**EXP:** A hormone activates a G protein-coupled receptor; Gαs stimulates adenylyl cyclase to form cAMP from ATP. cAMP activates protein kinase A and can also regulate cyclic nucleotide-gated channels and exchange proteins. PKA phosphorylation changes target enzyme and transcription-factor activity. Phosphodiesterases degrade cAMP to terminate the signal.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q642 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Explain 2): Explain the role of G proteins in signal transduction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Explain 2
**EXP:** Heterotrimeric G proteins couple activated membrane receptors to effectors. Receptor activation promotes GDP-GTP exchange on Gα; Gα-GTP and/or Gβγ then regulate adenylyl cyclase, phospholipase C, ion channels, or other effectors. Intrinsic GTP hydrolysis returns Gα to its inactive GDP-bound state and terminates the signal.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q643 (Hormone Action and Signal Transduction, written source p. 125, Explain 3): Explain the fate of the hormone-receptor complex.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 125, Explain 3
**EXP:** The fate depends on receptor class. Cell-surface receptor complexes may be internalized; the hormone can be degraded while the receptor is recycled, or both can be degraded, causing down-regulation. Intracellular hormone-receptor complexes regulate transcription and later dissociate; the hormone is metabolized and the receptor is recycled or degraded.
**Source Pages:** 125
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q666 (Carbohydrate Chemistry, written source p. 12, Explain 2): Explain why polysaccharides are considered non-reducing.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 12, Explain 2
**EXP:** Most anomeric carbons are tied up in glycosidic bonds. A polymer molecule has at most one reducing end, so its reducing activity is negligible relative to its size.

### Q697 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 1): Explain why phospholipids are amphipathic.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q698 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 2): Explain why snake bites may be toxic or fatal.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 2
**EXP:** Some venoms contain phospholipase A2, which hydrolyzes membrane phospholipids and generates lysophospholipids and fatty acids, damaging membranes and causing hemolysis, inflammation, and tissue injury.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q699 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 3): Explain how cholesterol moderates cell-membrane fluidity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 3
**EXP:** At higher temperatures cholesterol restrains phospholipid movement; at lower temperatures it prevents tight packing. It buffers membrane fluidity across temperatures.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q700 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 4): Explain why phospholipids are important in signal transduction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 4
**EXP:** Phospholipase C cleaves PIP2 to form IP3 and DAG. IP3 releases Ca2+ from the endoplasmic reticulum; DAG activates protein kinase C.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q701 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 5): Explain why phospholipid deficiency can cause respiratory distress syndrome.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 5
**EXP:** Dipalmitoyl phosphatidylcholine is the principal pulmonary surfactant phospholipid. Deficiency raises alveolar surface tension, promotes alveolar collapse, and impairs lung expansion.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q702 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 6): Explain why cholesterol is an important sterol.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 6
**EXP:** It is a membrane component and the precursor of bile acids, steroid hormones, and vitamin D; excess circulating cholesterol contributes to atherosclerosis.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q703 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 7): State the biomedical importance of trans fatty acids.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 7
**EXP:** Trans fats raise LDL cholesterol and can lower HDL cholesterol, increasing atherosclerotic cardiovascular risk.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q704 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 8): State the biomedical importance of polyunsaturated fatty acids.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 8
**EXP:** Linoleic and α-linolenic acids are essential dietary fatty acids. PUFAs are membrane components and eicosanoid precursors; omega-3 PUFAs can favor less inflammatory mediator profiles.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q705 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 9): Explain how IP3 and DAG participate in intracellular signal transduction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 9
**EXP:** Phospholipase C cleaves PIP2 into IP3 and DAG. IP3 triggers Ca2+ release from the endoplasmic reticulum; DAG remains in the membrane and, with Ca2+, activates protein kinase C.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q706 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 10): Explain why α-linolenic and linoleic acids must be supplied in the diet.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 10
**EXP:** Humans lack Δ12 and Δ15 desaturases, so they cannot introduce the omega-6 and omega-3 double bonds needed to synthesize linoleic and α-linolenic acids.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q707 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 11): Explain why phospholipids are important constituents of cell membranes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 11
**EXP:** Their amphipathic structure drives bilayer formation, creating a selective-permeability barrier and a matrix for membrane proteins and signaling molecules.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q708 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 12): Explain why lipids are important dietary constituents.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 12
**EXP:** They provide concentrated energy, essential fatty acids, and a vehicle for absorption of vitamins A, D, E, and K; they also supply membrane components and promote satiety.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q709 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 13): Explain why phospholipids are important in blood clotting.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 13
**EXP:** Activated platelets expose anionic phosphatidylserine, which provides a surface for assembly of coagulation enzyme complexes and accelerates thrombin generation.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

---

### Q710 (Lipid Chemistry, written source p. 18, Explain 14): Explain why glycolipids are important compounds.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 18, Explain 14
**EXP:** Glycolipids are membrane components, especially abundant in nervous tissue; their carbohydrate groups contribute to cell recognition, adhesion, receptor interactions, and blood-group antigens.
**Source Pages:** 18
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Lipid Chemistry

### Q728 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 1): Explain why denaturation is associated with decreased protein solubility and increased digestibility.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 1
**EXP:** Unfolding exposes hydrophobic regions that aggregate, lowering solubility. It also exposes peptide bonds to proteases, increasing digestibility; the primary structure is usually not cleaved by denaturation itself.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q729 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 2): Explain why proteins are amphoteric.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 2
**EXP:** Proteins contain ionizable amino, carboxyl, and side-chain groups. They can accept or donate protons and exist as zwitterions depending on pH.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q730 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 3): State the major effects of protein denaturation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 3
**EXP:** It disrupts secondary, tertiary, and quaternary structure, usually without breaking peptide bonds; biologic activity is lost, solubility may fall with coagulation, and proteolytic susceptibility often increases.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q731 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 4): Explain why some amino acids are polar despite having uncharged side chains.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 4
**EXP:** Their side chains contain polar groups such as hydroxyl or amide groups that form hydrogen bonds but carry no net charge at physiologic pH; examples include serine, threonine, asparagine, and glutamine.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q732 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 5): Explain why arginine is considered semi-essential.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 5
**EXP:** Arginine can be synthesized through the urea-cycle pathway, but production may not meet demand during growth or severe stress, so dietary supply can become conditionally necessary.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q733 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 6): State the biological importance of proteins.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 6
**EXP:** Proteins provide structure; catalyze reactions; transport and store molecules; mediate movement, signaling, and immunity; maintain acid-base balance and plasma oncotic pressure; and can serve as an energy source.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q734 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 25, Explain 7): Explain why an amino acid at its isoelectric point does not migrate in an electric field.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 25, Explain 7
**EXP:** At the isoelectric point its positive and negative charges balance, giving a net charge of zero; therefore it has no net electrophoretic migration.
**Source Pages:** 25
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q735 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 26, Compare 1): Compare essential and non-essential amino acids, giving examples.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 26, Compare 1
**EXP:** Essential amino acids cannot be synthesized in sufficient amounts and must be obtained from diet (for example, leucine or lysine). Non-essential amino acids can normally be synthesized in the body (for example, alanine or glutamate); some become conditionally essential during growth or illness.
**Source Pages:** 26
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q736 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 26, Compare 2): Compare proteins of high and low biological value.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 26, Compare 2
**EXP:** High-biological-value proteins provide all indispensable amino acids in adequate proportions and are generally highly digestible (for example, egg or milk proteins). Low-biological-value proteins have one or more limiting essential amino acids or lower digestibility (for example, zein from maize). Complementary plant proteins can improve amino-acid balance.
**Source Pages:** 26
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q737 (Protein Chemistry, written source p. 26, Compare 3): Compare primary and secondary protein structure, including stabilizing bonds.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 26, Compare 3
**EXP:** Primary structure is the amino-acid sequence joined by covalent peptide bonds. Secondary structure is local folding into α-helices or β-sheets, stabilized mainly by hydrogen bonds between backbone C=O and N-H groups.
**Source Pages:** 26
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

## Heme Metabolism: written questions from neonatal jaundice case

### Q747 (Heme Metabolism, written source Case 1, Q1): What is the most probable diagnosis?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** PDF p. 115, Case 1, Q1
**EXP:** A newborn with jaundice, elevated serum bilirubin, and hemolytic anemia most likely has hemolytic (prehepatic) jaundice. Hemolysis increases bilirubin production beyond the liver’s conjugating capacity, causing unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q748 (Heme Metabolism, written source Case 1, Q2): Which type of bilirubin is expected to be elevated, conjugated or unconjugated?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** PDF p. 115, Case 1, Q2
**EXP:** The predominantly increased fraction is unconjugated (indirect) bilirubin because heme breakdown raises bilirubin production and neonatal conjugating capacity is limited.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q749 (Heme Metabolism, written source Case 1, Q3): Comment on the expected color of urine.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** PDF p. 115, Case 1, Q3
**EXP:** Urine may be dark from increased urobilinogen/urobilin, but unconjugated bilirubin is albumin-bound and is not filtered into urine; therefore bilirubinuria is absent.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q750 (Heme Metabolism, written source Case 1, Q4): How can this condition be treated?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** PDF p. 115, Case 1, Q4
**EXP:** Use phototherapy for significant unconjugated neonatal hyperbilirubinemia. Blue light converts bilirubin into more water-soluble photoisomers that can be excreted without hepatic conjugation; treat the hemolytic cause and use exchange transfusion if severe or indicated.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

## Written prompts recovered from enzyme and heme-metabolism pages 31 and 115

### Q751 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Define 1): Define zymogen.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Define 1
**EXP:** An inactive enzyme precursor activated by a specific step, commonly proteolytic cleavage (for example, trypsinogen to trypsin).
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q752 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Define 2): Define Km.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Define 2
**EXP:** In Michaelis–Menten kinetics, Km is the substrate concentration at which velocity is one-half Vmax.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q753 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Define 3): Define an allosteric inhibitor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Define 3
**EXP:** A molecule that binds a regulatory site distinct from the active site and changes enzyme conformation or activity.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q754 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Define 4): Define a competitive inhibitor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Define 4
**EXP:** A molecule that competes with substrate for the active site; it increases apparent Km while Vmax remains unchanged.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q755 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 1): Name three factors affecting enzyme activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Temperature, pH, and substrate concentration; enzyme concentration, cofactors, and inhibitors also affect the rate.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q756 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 2): Give two examples of competitive enzyme inhibitors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Methotrexate inhibits dihydrofolate reductase; statins inhibit HMG-CoA reductase. Sulfonamides are another competitive PABA analog.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q757 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 3): Name two isoenzymes increased in myocardial infarction.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** CK-MB and LDH-1 are classic examples; cardiac troponins are more specific contemporary markers.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q758 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 4): Give two irreversible enzyme inhibitors that act on cofactors or prosthetic groups.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Cyanide binds the heme of cytochrome c oxidase; arsenite binds lipoic-acid-containing enzyme complexes.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q759 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 5): State characteristics of isoenzymes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 5
**EXP:** They catalyze the same reaction but differ in structure, tissue distribution, physicochemical/electrophoretic properties, and often kinetic or immunologic features; these differences aid diagnosis.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q760 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Enumerate 6): Give two irreversible inhibitors that act directly on enzymes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Enumerate 6
**EXP:** Aspirin irreversibly acetylates cyclooxygenase; organophosphates irreversibly inhibit acetylcholinesterase.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q761 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 1): Explain why enzyme activity stops at about 70 °C.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 1
**EXP:** High temperature disrupts the noncovalent interactions maintaining enzyme conformation, denaturing the protein and distorting its active site.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q762 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 2): Explain why cyanides are highly toxic.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 2
**EXP:** Cyanide inhibits cytochrome c oxidase (complex IV), blocking electron transfer to oxygen and oxidative phosphorylation and causing histotoxic hypoxia.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q763 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 3): Explain why sulfanilamide is bacteriostatic.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 3
**EXP:** It mimics PABA and competitively inhibits bacterial dihydropteroate synthase, preventing folate synthesis and bacterial growth.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q764 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 4): Explain why dicumarol is an anticoagulant.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 4
**EXP:** It inhibits vitamin K epoxide reductase, reducing active vitamin K and γ-carboxylation of clotting factors II, VII, IX, and X and proteins C and S.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q765 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 5): Explain why aspirin is anti-inflammatory.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 5
**EXP:** Aspirin irreversibly acetylates cyclooxygenase, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis and reducing inflammation, pain, and fever.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q766 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Explain 6): Explain why allopurinol is used to treat gout.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Explain 6
**EXP:** Allopurinol/oxypurinol inhibit xanthine oxidase, decreasing conversion of hypoxanthine and xanthine to uric acid.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q767 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Compare 1): Compare competitive and allosteric enzyme inhibitors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Compare 1
**EXP:** Competitive inhibitors bind the active site and raise apparent Km without changing Vmax; allosteric inhibitors bind a separate regulatory site and change enzyme conformation, often altering Vmax or apparent affinity.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q768 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Compare 2): Compare a repressor and an enzyme inhibitor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Compare 2
**EXP:** A repressor binds DNA regulatory sequences and reduces transcription; an enzyme inhibitor binds an enzyme and reduces catalytic activity.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q769 (Enzymes, written source p. 31, Compare 3): Compare enzyme inhibition by H₂O₂ with inhibition of xanthine oxidase by allopurinol.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 31, Compare 3
**EXP:** H₂O₂ can oxidatively damage residues/cofactors and irreversibly reduce activity. Allopurinol is a substrate analog converted to oxypurinol, which tightly inhibits xanthine oxidase.
**Source Pages:** 31
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q770 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 1): Explain why porphyrias are grouped into acute neurovisceral and cutaneous forms.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 1
**EXP:** Acute hepatic porphyrias accumulate precursors such as ALA/PBG and cause neurovisceral attacks; cutaneous porphyrias accumulate photosensitizing porphyrins in skin and cause light-induced injury.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q771 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 2): Explain why lead exposure can cause anemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 2
**EXP:** Lead inhibits ALA dehydratase and ferrochelatase, impairing heme synthesis; it also shortens red-cell survival.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q772 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 3): Explain why phenobarbital has been used for physiologic neonatal jaundice.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 3
**EXP:** Phenobarbital induces hepatic UDP-glucuronosyltransferase and can increase bilirubin conjugation; phototherapy is the usual treatment for significant unconjugated neonatal jaundice.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q773 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 4): Explain why blue-light phototherapy treats physiologic neonatal jaundice.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 4
**EXP:** Blue light converts unconjugated bilirubin to more water-soluble photoisomers that can be excreted without hepatic glucuronidation.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q774 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 5): Explain why barbiturates and sulfonamides can precipitate porphyria attacks.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 5
**EXP:** Some drugs induce hepatic cytochrome P450 and increase heme demand, stimulating ALAS1 and causing toxic porphyrin precursors to accumulate behind a pathway block.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q775 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 115, Explain 6): Explain why PLP deficiency can cause anemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 115, Explain 6
**EXP:** PLP is the cofactor for erythroid ALA synthase, the first step in heme synthesis; deficiency reduces heme production and may cause sideroblastic/microcytic anemia.
**Source Pages:** 115
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

## Written prompts recovered from general-protein pages 57–59

### Q776 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Define 1): Define biological value of a protein.

**Answer Source:** derived

**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q777 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Define 2): Define nitrogen balance.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Define 2
**EXP:** Nitrogen intake, mainly from protein, minus nitrogen losses in urine, feces, and other routes.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q778 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Define 3): Define transamination.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Define 3
**EXP:** Transfer of an amino group from an amino acid to an α-keto acid, forming a new amino acid and keto acid; aminotransferases require PLP.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q779 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 1): Write a short note on celiac disease: cause, symptoms, and treatment.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 1
**EXP:** Celiac disease is immune-mediated small-intestinal injury triggered by gluten in genetically susceptible people. Villous atrophy causes malabsorption, diarrhea, weight loss, anemia, or extraintestinal signs. Treat with a strict lifelong gluten-free diet and correct nutrient deficiencies.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q780 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 2): Write a short note on nitrogen balance: definition and types.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 2
**EXP:** Nitrogen balance is intake minus loss. Positive balance occurs in growth, pregnancy, or recovery; zero balance in a healthy adult at steady state; negative balance in starvation, fever, trauma, or severe illness.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q781 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 3): Write a short note on the diagnostic value of plasma aminotransferases.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 3
**EXP:** ALT and AST rise with hepatocellular injury. ALT is more liver-associated; AST is also present in heart and skeletal muscle. They indicate cellular injury, not hepatic synthetic function.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q782 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 4): Write a short note on the catabolic and excretory fate of ammonia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 4
**EXP:** Ammonia is incorporated into glutamate/glutamine for transport; hepatic urea-cycle conversion provides the major route of disposal. Renal ammonium excretion is another route.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q783 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 5): Write a short note on types of hyperammonemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 5
**EXP:** Primary hyperammonemia results from inherited urea-cycle defects. Secondary hyperammonemia occurs with liver failure or metabolic disorders such as organic acidemias that impair ureagenesis or increase ammonia production.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q784 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 57, Short note 6): Write a short note on regulation of the urea cycle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 57, Short note 6
**EXP:** CPS1 is activated by N-acetylglutamate, whose synthesis is stimulated by arginine. High protein intake and catabolic states increase urea-cycle enzyme expression; substrate and liver function also affect flux.
**Source Pages:** 57
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 58, Mention 1
**EXP:** Positive: pregnancy or growth. Zero: healthy adult at steady state. Negative: starvation, fever, trauma, or severe illness.
**Source Pages:** 58
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q807 (General Protein Metabolism, written source p. 58, Explain 7): Explain why ammonia remains at very low levels in blood despite continuous tissue production.

## Written prompts recovered from TCA-cycle pages 37–39

### Q820 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Matching A): Match each Krebs-cycle enzyme with the listed property: (1) succinate dehydrogenase; (2) α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase; (3) malate dehydrogenase; (4) malic enzyme. Choices: (a) needs TPP as a cofactor; (b) is a component of the electron transport chain; (c) is inhibited by fluoroacetate; (d) produces NADH; (e) produces NADPH.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Matching A
**EXP:** 1-b, 2-a, 3-d, 4-e. Succinate dehydrogenase is Complex II; α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase uses thiamine pyrophosphate; malate dehydrogenase reduces NAD⁺ to NADH; malic enzyme produces NADPH.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q821 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Matching B): Match each substance with its listed effect: (1) thermogenin; (2) dinitrophenol; (3) cyanide; (4) fluoroacetate. Choices: (a) inhibits the Krebs cycle; (b) inhibits cytochrome c oxidase; (c) uncoupling protein; (d) activates the Krebs cycle; (e) increases inner-mitochondrial-membrane permeability to protons.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Matching B
**EXP:** 1-c, 2-e, 3-b, 4-a. Thermogenin is UCP1; dinitrophenol dissipates the proton gradient; cyanide inhibits Complex IV; fluoroacetate is converted to fluorocitrate, which inhibits aconitase.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q834 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 1): Explain why the citric acid cycle has many biological functions in addition to energy production.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 1
**EXP:** The cycle is amphibolic. Its intermediates supply biosynthesis: citrate supports fatty-acid and cholesterol synthesis; α-ketoglutarate and oxaloacetate supply amino-acid synthesis; succinyl-CoA supplies heme synthesis; and oxaloacetate/malate can support gluconeogenesis. Anaplerotic reactions replenish intermediates removed for these pathways.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q835 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 2): Explain why the P/O ratio may be 3/1 or 2/1.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 2
**EXP:** The source uses the classical theoretical convention: oxidation of one mitochondrial NADH was assigned about 3 ATP, whereas FADH₂ entering at Complex II was assigned about 2 ATP. Thus the ratios are 3 and 2, respectively (often written per oxygen atom reduced). Modern measured estimates are lower, about 2.5 and 1.5 ATP.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q836 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 3): Explain why carbon monoxide and cyanide are toxic to tissues.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 3
**EXP:** Both inhibit cytochrome c oxidase (Complex IV) and prevent transfer of electrons to oxygen. Oxidative phosphorylation stops, ATP falls, and tissues develop cellular hypoxia despite oxygen being present in blood.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q837 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 4): Explain why regulation of the Krebs cycle is related to respiratory-chain activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 4
**EXP:** The cycle depends on NAD⁺ and FAD to oxidize its substrates. The respiratory chain reoxidizes NADH and FADH₂; when electron transport is slow, NADH accumulates and NAD⁺ falls, inhibiting dehydrogenases. ADP availability and ATP demand also coordinate both pathways.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q838 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 5): State important functions of the carbon dioxide released through the Krebs cycle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 5
**EXP:** CO₂ contributes to the bicarbonate buffer and transport of carbon dioxide from tissues to lungs; it also participates in carboxylation reactions and carbamoyl-phosphate synthesis. The wording is broad, so these are representative physiological and metabolic roles.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q839 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 6): Explain how calcium increases the rate of the Krebs cycle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 6
**EXP:** In active muscle, Ca²⁺ activates pyruvate dehydrogenase indirectly and stimulates isocitrate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase. This increases production of NADH for ATP generation during contraction.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q840 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 7): Explain how uncoupling oxidative phosphorylation can be biologically useful.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 7
**EXP:** Uncoupling proteins allow protons to return across the inner mitochondrial membrane without ATP synthesis. The gradient energy is released as heat; UCP1 in brown adipose tissue supports non-shivering thermogenesis.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q841 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 8): Explain how ADP availability regulates the rate of electron transport.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 8
**EXP:** ADP is the substrate for ATP synthase. More ADP drives proton flow through ATP synthase, dissipates the proton gradient, and permits faster electron transport and oxygen consumption (acceptor control). When ADP is scarce, the gradient builds and respiration slows.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q842 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 9): Explain why dinitrophenol is an uncoupler.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 9
**EXP:** Dinitrophenol is a lipid-soluble weak acid that carries protons across the inner mitochondrial membrane, collapsing the proton-motive force. Electron transport and oxygen consumption may continue, but ATP synthesis falls and energy is released as heat.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q843 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 10): Explain how cyanide inhibits the electron transport chain.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 10
**EXP:** Cyanide binds the heme a₃/CuB center of cytochrome c oxidase (Complex IV), blocking reduction of oxygen to water. Electron flow, proton pumping, and oxidative phosphorylation then stop.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q844 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Explain 11): Explain why muscular exercise stimulates the Krebs cycle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Explain 11
**EXP:** Contraction raises ADP, AMP, and Ca²⁺ and increases delivery of pyruvate and fatty-acid-derived acetyl-CoA. These signals activate oxidative metabolism and increase Krebs-cycle flux to produce NADH/FADH₂ for ATP synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q845 (Bioenergetics & TCA Cycle, written source pp. 37–39, Compare 1): Compare cyanide with dinitrophenol as inhibitors of oxidative phosphorylation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 37–39, Compare 1
**EXP:** Cyanide is a respiratory-chain poison: it blocks Complex IV, stopping electron transfer to oxygen, oxygen consumption, and ATP synthesis. Dinitrophenol is an uncoupler: it dissipates the proton gradient, so electron transport and oxygen consumption can continue or rise while ATP synthesis decreases; both are toxic, but by different mechanisms.
**Source Pages:** 37–39
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q847 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Define 1): Define hypoglycemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Define 1
**EXP:** A plasma glucose concentration below the normal physiological range; in adults, <70 mg/dL is commonly used as an alert threshold, while clinical interpretation depends on symptoms and context.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q848 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Define 2): Define gluconeogenesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Define 2
**EXP:** Synthesis of glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors, mainly lactate, glycerol, and glucogenic amino acids, principally in liver and also kidney.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q849 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Define 3): Define glucosuria.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Define 3
**EXP:** Presence of glucose in urine, usually because plasma glucose exceeds renal reabsorptive capacity or because tubular reabsorption is impaired.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q850 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Define 4): Define renal glucosuria.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Define 4
**EXP:** Urinary glucose excretion despite a normal or near-normal plasma glucose concentration, usually from reduced proximal-tubular glucose reabsorption.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q859 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Enumerate 9): Give two genetic causes of fasting hypoglycemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

### Q860 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Enumerate 10): Give two causes of postprandial hypoglycemia.

**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Enumerate 10

**Source Pages:** 46–48

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q861 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Enumerate 11): Give two causes of hyperglycemic glucosuria.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Enumerate 11
**EXP:** Diabetes mellitus and stress or endocrine hyperglycemia (for example, catecholamine excess) can raise plasma glucose above the renal threshold.
**Source Pages:** 46–48

---

### Q863 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 1): Explain why cellulose, although non-digestible, is an important dietary component.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 1
**EXP:** Humans lack cellulase, so cellulose acts as dietary fiber. It adds bulk, retains water, supports bowel regularity, and can be fermented partly by colonic microbiota.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q864 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 2): Explain why lactose intolerance causes vomiting, watery diarrhea, and colic after lactose ingestion.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 2
**EXP:** Lactase deficiency leaves lactose unabsorbed in the intestinal lumen. Its osmotic effect draws water into the bowel, while bacterial fermentation produces acids and gas, causing diarrhea, bloating, and cramps; nausea or vomiting may accompany severe symptoms.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q865 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 3): Explain why oxidation of even-chain fatty acids does not produce net glucose.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 3
**EXP:** Even-chain fatty acids yield acetyl-CoA. In mammals, pyruvate dehydrogenase is irreversible and acetyl-CoA carbons are lost as CO₂ in the TCA cycle, so they cannot provide net oxaloacetate or glucose.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q866 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 4): Explain why pheochromocytoma may be complicated by diabetes or hyperglycemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 4
**EXP:** Excess catecholamines stimulate hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, inhibit insulin secretion, and promote glucagon action, raising blood glucose.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q867 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 5): Explain why insulin is a hypoglycemic hormone.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 5
**EXP:** Insulin increases glucose uptake in insulin-sensitive muscle and adipose tissue, promotes glycogenesis and glycolysis, and suppresses hepatic glucose production through inhibition of glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q868 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 6): Explain why favism is associated with hemolytic anemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 6
**EXP:** In G6PD deficiency, red cells cannot generate enough NADPH through the oxidative pentose-phosphate pathway to keep glutathione reduced. Oxidant exposure damages hemoglobin and membranes, causing hemolysis.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q869 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 7): Explain how muscle can synthesize nucleotides despite having relatively low G6PD/HMP-pathway activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 7
**EXP:** Ribose-5-phosphate can be formed by running the non-oxidative pentose-phosphate reactions from glycolytic intermediates in the reverse direction. NADPH can also come from other cellular reactions, including malic enzyme and NADP-dependent isocitrate dehydrogenase.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q870 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 8): Explain why the HMP (pentose-phosphate) pathway is important in red blood cells.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 8
**EXP:** It is the red cell’s principal source of NADPH, which maintains reduced glutathione and protects hemoglobin and the membrane from oxidant injury. It also provides ribose-5-phosphate for nucleotide synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q871 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 9): Explain why thiamine (vitamin B₁) deficiency can produce lactic acidosis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 9
**EXP:** Thiamine pyrophosphate is required by pyruvate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase. Reduced oxidative use of pyruvate diverts it toward lactate, which can accumulate.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q872 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 10): Explain why primaquine, aspirin, or fava beans can precipitate favism/hemolysis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 10
**EXP:** These exposures increase oxidative stress. In G6PD-deficient red cells, NADPH and reduced glutathione are insufficient to neutralize oxidants, leading to hemoglobin and membrane damage and hemolysis. The risk varies by drug, dose, and patient.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q873 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 11): Explain why muscle glycogen cannot directly maintain blood glucose.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 11
**EXP:** Muscle lacks glucose-6-phosphatase. Its glycogen-derived glucose-6-phosphate is used locally in glycolysis rather than converted to free glucose for release into blood.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q874 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 12): Explain why fasting hypoglycemia is a principal metabolic effect of Von Gierke disease.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 12
**EXP:** Glucose-6-phosphatase-system deficiency prevents the final release of free glucose from hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, so blood glucose falls during fasting.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q875 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 13): Explain why Von Gierke disease can cause gout (hyperuricemia).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 13
**EXP:** Accumulated glucose-6-phosphate is diverted into the pentose-phosphate pathway, increasing ribose-5-phosphate/PRPP and purine synthesis and breakdown. Lactic acidosis also reduces renal urate excretion by competing with urate.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q876 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Explain 14): Explain why Von Gierke disease can cause hypertriacylglycerolemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Explain 14
**EXP:** Excess glucose-6-phosphate is diverted into glycolysis and de novo fatty-acid synthesis; increased hepatic triacylglycerol production and VLDL export raise plasma triacylglycerol.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Deficient enzyme 1
**EXP:** Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD).
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Deficient enzyme 2
**EXP:** Intestinal brush-border lactase (lactase-phlorizin hydrolase).
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**EXP:** Glucose-6-phosphatase in type Ia; defects in the glucose-6-phosphate transport system cause related type Ib disease. The source does not specify a subtype.

**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Key enzyme 2

### Q885 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Compare 1): Compare hexokinase with glucokinase.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Compare 1
**EXP:** Hexokinase is present in most tissues, has a low Km (high affinity) and low Vmax, acts on several hexoses, and is inhibited by glucose-6-phosphate. Glucokinase is mainly in liver and pancreatic β cells, has a higher Km and higher capacity, is specific for glucose, is not inhibited by glucose-6-phosphate, and is induced by insulin; it helps clear postprandial glucose and sense blood glucose.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q886 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 1a): A 10-year-old boy has recurrent fasting weakness, sweating and pallor relieved by eating, hepatomegaly, hypoglycemia, acidosis, and dyslipidemia; enzyme testing confirms Von Gierke disease. What is the enzymatic defect?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 1a
**EXP:** Type Ia Von Gierke disease is due to glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency. The source does not identify a subtype, so a defect in the glucose-6-phosphatase system is the safest broader answer; type Ib involves the G6P transporter.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q887 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 1b): In the described child with Von Gierke disease, enumerate the biochemical changes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 1b
**EXP:** Fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, hypertriacylglycerolemia, and often hypercholesterolemia; hepatomegaly reflects glycogen and fat accumulation.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q888 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 1c): Why is the described child with Von Gierke disease at high risk for gout?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 1c
**EXP:** Increased purine synthesis and degradation raises urate, while lactic acidosis competes with urate for renal excretion. Both mechanisms promote hyperuricemia and gout.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q889 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 1d): Mention two non-genetic causes of fasting hypoglycemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 1d
**EXP:** Severe liver disease and adrenal insufficiency are two causes. Alcohol use, sepsis, and prolonged malnutrition are other examples.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q890 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 2a): A 2-month-old infant develops vomiting, watery diarrhea, and colic after breastfeeding. What is the diagnosis?

**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 2a

### Q891 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 2b): In the infant with symptoms after breastfeeding, what causes these symptoms?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 2b
**EXP:** Insufficient intestinal lactase leaves milk lactose undigested. Unabsorbed lactose causes osmotic diarrhea and bacterial fermentation produces acids and gas, leading to colic and sometimes vomiting.
**Source Pages:** 46–48
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q892 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 2c): How can the infant with lactose intolerance be treated?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 2c
**EXP:** Use lactose-free or lactose-reduced infant formula and ensure adequate nutrition; lactase supplementation may be considered. Breastfeeding and treatment should be individualized with pediatric guidance.

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q893 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 3a): A child with chronic hemolytic anemia develops acute hemolysis after sulfonamide exposure; the source attributes this to a defective HMP pathway. Name the enzymatic defect.

### Q894 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 3b): For the child with HMP-pathway deficiency, name two important sites where the pathway is active.

**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 3b

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q895 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 3c): For the child with HMP-pathway deficiency, state two functions of the pathway.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

---

### Q896 (Carbohydrate Metabolism, written source pp. 46–48, Case 3d): Explain the biochemical basis of hemolysis in the child with HMP-pathway deficiency.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 46–48, Case 3d
**EXP:** G6PD-deficient red cells have inadequate NADPH to regenerate reduced glutathione. Oxidants then denature hemoglobin and injure the membrane, causing intravascular or extravascular hemolysis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 8
