---

### Q906 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 9): Give three differences between depot fat and tissue fat.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 9
**EXP:** Depot fat is chiefly triacylglycerol stored in adipocytes as an energy reserve; tissue/structural fat is incorporated into membranes and other cellular structures and is richer in phospholipids and cholesterol. Depot fat is mobilized during fasting, whereas structural lipid is not a major rapidly mobilizable energy store. Their locations and fatty-acid composition also differ.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q907 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 10): Name the enzyme deficient in Refsum disease.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 10
**EXP:** Phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase, the peroxisomal enzyme for α-oxidation of phytanic acid.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q908 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 11): State the importance of ketone bodies.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 11
**EXP:** They are water-soluble fuels made by liver from fatty-acid-derived acetyl-CoA and used by extrahepatic tissues, including heart, muscle, and—during prolonged fasting—the brain. They help spare glucose and body protein.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q909 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 12): Name ketogenic substances.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 12
**EXP:** Fatty acids and the exclusively ketogenic amino acids leucine and lysine; acetyl-CoA and ketogenic amino-acid carbon skeletons are precursors.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q910 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 13): Name a drug that blocks leukotriene action.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 13
**EXP:** Montelukast (Singulair), a cysteinyl-leukotriene CysLT₁ receptor antagonist.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q911 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 14): Compare the mechanisms of steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (SAIDs) and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 14
**EXP:** Glucocorticoids induce annexin-1 and suppress phospholipase A₂-dependent arachidonic-acid release, while also reducing inflammatory gene expression. NSAIDs inhibit cyclooxygenase (COX), lowering prostaglandin and thromboxane synthesis; they do not directly block leukotriene synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q912 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 15): Give three causes of ketosis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 15
**EXP:** Prolonged fasting or starvation, uncontrolled insulin-deficient diabetes, and a very-low-carbohydrate/high-fat diet are causes. Pregnancy or prolonged vomiting can also predispose.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q913 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 16): State the importance of β-oxidation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 16
**EXP:** It generates NADH and FADH₂ for oxidative phosphorylation and acetyl-CoA for the TCA cycle or ketogenesis, providing a major source of ATP during fasting and exercise.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q914 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 17): State the mechanism of action of Singulair.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 17
**EXP:** Montelukast selectively antagonizes the CysLT₁ receptor, reducing leukotriene-mediated bronchoconstriction, mucus secretion, vascular permeability, and airway inflammation.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q915 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 18): Describe regulation of β-oxidation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 18
**EXP:** Fatty-acid supply rises with catecholamine-stimulated lipolysis and falls with insulin. Mitochondrial entry of long-chain fatty acids is controlled by CPT-I, which is inhibited by malonyl-CoA; fasting lowers malonyl-CoA and favors oxidation.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q916 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 19): Describe hormonal regulation of fatty-acid synthesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 19
**EXP:** Insulin promotes dephosphorylation/activation of acetyl-CoA carboxylase and induces lipogenic enzymes. Glucagon and epinephrine signal through phosphorylation to inhibit ACC and reduce fatty-acid synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q917 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Mention 20): Describe hormonal regulation of lipolysis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Mention 20
**EXP:** Catecholamines activate adipocyte cAMP/PKA signaling, which phosphorylates perilipin and hormone-sensitive lipase and promotes triacylglycerol breakdown. Insulin suppresses lipolysis by lowering cAMP and promoting dephosphorylation; its effect is dominant in the fed state.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q918 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 1): Name the key enzyme(s) of lipolysis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 1
**EXP:** Adipose triglyceride lipase initiates triacylglycerol hydrolysis, hormone-sensitive lipase mainly hydrolyzes diacylglycerol, and monoacylglycerol lipase completes the pathway.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q919 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 2): Name the key enzyme(s) of lipogenesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 2
**EXP:** Acetyl-CoA carboxylase forms malonyl-CoA and is the principal regulatory enzyme; fatty-acid synthase builds the fatty-acid chain.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q920 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 3): Name the key enzyme(s) of ketogenesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Key enzyme 3
**EXP:** Mitochondrial HMG-CoA synthase is the rate-limiting enzyme, followed by HMG-CoA lyase.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q921 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 1): Explain why lipid emulsification is important for pancreatic-lipase action.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 1
**EXP:** Emulsification by bile salts breaks large fat globules into small droplets, greatly increasing the lipid–water interfacial area accessible to pancreatic lipase. Colipase anchors the enzyme at that interface.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q922 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 2): Explain why heparin is called the clearing factor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 2
**EXP:** Heparin releases endothelial-bound lipoprotein lipase into plasma. The enzyme hydrolyzes triacylglycerol in chylomicrons and VLDL, clearing lipemic plasma; this historical effect led to the term “clearing factor.”
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q923 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 3): Explain how overfeeding carbohydrate can lead to obesity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 3
**EXP:** When carbohydrate intake exceeds immediate energy needs and glycogen-storage capacity, glycolytic carbon is converted to acetyl-CoA and fatty acids, then triacylglycerol. Insulin favors this storage, increasing adipose mass over time.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q924 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 4): Explain why α-linolenic acid and linoleic acid are essential fatty acids.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 4
**EXP:** Humans cannot introduce double bonds beyond the Δ9 position, so they cannot synthesize linoleic acid (ω-6) or α-linolenic acid (ω-3) de novo; both must be obtained from the diet.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q925 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 5): Explain why adipose tissue cannot effectively use free glycerol for triacylglycerol synthesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 5
**EXP:** Adipocytes have very low glycerol-kinase activity, so they cannot efficiently phosphorylate free glycerol to glycerol-3-phosphate. They mainly form glycerol-3-phosphate from the glycolytic intermediate dihydroxyacetone phosphate.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q926 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 6): Explain why ketogenesis occurs in liver whereas ketolysis occurs in extrahepatic tissues.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 6
**EXP:** Liver mitochondria express the ketogenesis enzymes, including HMG-CoA synthase and HMG-CoA lyase. Liver lacks succinyl-CoA:acetoacetate CoA transferase (SCOT/thiophorase), so it cannot oxidize ketone bodies; extrahepatic tissues express SCOT and use ketones as fuel.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q927 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 7): Explain how aspirin helps prevent platelet aggregation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 7
**EXP:** Aspirin irreversibly acetylates platelet COX-1, decreasing thromboxane A₂ synthesis. Because platelets cannot resynthesize COX, their pro-aggregatory effect is reduced for the remainder of their lifespan.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q928 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Explain 8): Explain why ketolysis depends on activity of the citric-acid cycle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Explain 8
**EXP:** Ketolysis converts acetoacetate to acetyl-CoA, which must be oxidized in the TCA cycle. Adequate oxaloacetate and TCA-cycle flux are needed; during fasting, oxaloacetate is drawn toward gluconeogenesis, favoring ketone-body accumulation.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q929 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Compare 1): Compare tissue fat with depot fat.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Compare 1
**EXP:** Depot fat is stored mainly as triacylglycerol in adipocytes and serves as a mobilizable energy reserve. Tissue fat is structural lipid in cell membranes and organs, relatively rich in phospholipids and cholesterol, and is not mobilized as the main reserve.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q930 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Compare 2): Compare PGE₂ with PGF₂α.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Compare 2
**EXP:** Both are prostaglandins derived from arachidonic acid via COX. PGE₂ commonly mediates inflammation, pain, fever, vasodilation, and gastric/mucosal or renal effects. PGF₂α promotes uterine smooth-muscle contraction and can constrict bronchi and vessels; their receptor distributions give different tissue actions.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q931 (Lipid Metabolism, written source pp. 52–54, Compare 3): Compare prostacyclin I₂ (PGI₂) with thromboxane A₂ (TXA₂).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 52–54, Compare 3
**EXP:** PGI₂ is produced mainly by vascular endothelium; it vasodilates and inhibits platelet aggregation. TXA₂ is produced mainly by activated platelets; it vasoconstricts and promotes platelet aggregation. They have opposing effects on hemostasis and vascular tone.
**Source Pages:** 52–54
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q933 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Mention 1): State the importance of glutathione.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Mention 1
**EXP:** Glutathione is a major intracellular antioxidant that reduces peroxides and maintains protein sulfhydryl groups; it also participates in detoxification, amino-acid transport, and maintenance of red-cell integrity.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q934 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Mention 2): State the importance of glutamic acid.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Mention 2
**EXP:** Glutamate is a central amino-group acceptor/donor in transamination, a precursor of glutamine, GABA, and glutathione, and a major excitatory neurotransmitter.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q943 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Disease 1): For albinism, name the deficient enzyme and its manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Disease 1
**EXP:** Most commonly, tyrosinase deficiency impairs melanin synthesis, causing generalized or ocular hypopigmentation, photosensitivity, and visual abnormalities with increased skin-cancer risk. Some forms of albinism have other genetic causes.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q944 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Disease 2): For alkaptonuria, name the deficient enzyme and its manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Disease 2
**EXP:** Homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (homogentisic acid oxidase) deficiency causes homogentisic-acid accumulation, darkening of urine on standing, ochronosis, and degenerative arthropathy.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q945 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Disease 3): For phenylketonuria, name the deficient enzyme and its manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Disease 3
**EXP:** Usually phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency (or less commonly tetrahydrobiopterin-pathway defects). Untreated disease causes elevated phenylalanine/phenylketones, neurodevelopmental impairment, seizures or behavioral problems, and hypopigmentation.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q946 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Disease 4): For homocystinuria, name the deficient enzyme and its manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Disease 4
**EXP:** Classic homocystinuria is most often due to cystathionine β-synthase deficiency. Manifestations include elevated homocysteine, lens subluxation, marfanoid habitus, osteoporosis, developmental problems, and arterial/venous thrombosis.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q947 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Explain 1): Explain why glutamine is important in the synthesis of many compounds.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Explain 1
**EXP:** Glutamine safely transports ammonia and donates amide nitrogen in biosynthesis, including purines, pyrimidines, amino sugars, asparagine, and NAD⁺. It is also an energy and nitrogen source for rapidly dividing cells.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q948 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Explain 2): Explain the role of glutamic acid in activation of blood-clotting factors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Explain 2
**EXP:** Vitamin K-dependent γ-glutamyl carboxylase modifies selected glutamate residues in clotting-factor precursors to γ-carboxyglutamate (Gla). Glutamate supplies the residue being modified; glutamate itself is not the activating enzyme or cofactor.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q949 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Explain 3): Explain why untreated phenylketonuria can cause intellectual disability by about one year of age.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Explain 3
**EXP:** High phenylalanine and phenylketones disrupt brain development, compete with other large neutral amino acids for transport into the brain, and impair neurotransmitter and protein synthesis. Early dietary treatment prevents much of this injury.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q950 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Explain 4): Explain why patients with phenylketonuria may have hypopigmentation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Explain 4
**EXP:** Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency lowers tyrosine availability, reducing substrate for tyrosinase-dependent melanin synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q951 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Explain 5): Explain why pellagra may develop in a carcinoid tumor.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Explain 5
**EXP:** A serotonin-producing tumor diverts tryptophan away from niacin synthesis. If dietary intake and other sources cannot compensate, niacin deficiency and pellagra may result.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q952 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 1): Write a short note on hyperhomocysteinemia, including causes and effects.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 1
**EXP:** Causes include folate, vitamin B₁₂, or B₆ deficiency; renal disease; and inherited defects such as MTHFR or cystathionine β-synthase variants. Elevated homocysteine injures endothelium and is associated with increased thrombotic and vascular risk.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q953 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 2): Write a short note on homocystinuria, including causes and effects.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 2
**EXP:** Classic homocystinuria is usually autosomal-recessive cystathionine β-synthase deficiency, causing homocysteine and methionine accumulation with low cysteine. It may cause lens subluxation, marfanoid skeletal features, developmental impairment, and thrombosis. Management may include pyridoxine when responsive, folate/B₁₂, betaine, and methionine restriction with cysteine supplementation.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q954 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 3): Write a short note on cystinuria, including causes, effects, and treatment.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 3
**EXP:** Cystinuria is an inherited proximal-tubule/intestinal transport defect for cystine and dibasic amino acids (COLA: cystine, ornithine, lysine, arginine). Poorly soluble cystine forms recurrent stones. Treatment includes high fluid intake, reduced sodium, urine alkalinization, and thiol drugs such as tiopronin for recurrent stones.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q955 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 4): Write a short note on phenylketonuria, including cause, characteristics, and treatment.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 4
**EXP:** PKU is usually phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency, less often a tetrahydrobiopterin-pathway defect. Untreated disease causes high phenylalanine, phenylketonuria, neurodevelopmental injury, seizures/behavioral changes, and hypopigmentation. Screen early and start lifelong phenylalanine restriction with adequate tyrosine; selected patients may benefit from sapropterin or other specialist therapies.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q956 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 5): Write a short note on alkaptonuria, including cause and manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 5
**EXP:** Autosomal-recessive homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase deficiency causes homogentisic acid accumulation. Urine may darken on standing; pigment deposits in connective tissue cause ochronosis and progressive arthritis, sometimes with cardiac-valve or renal complications.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q957 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 6): Write a short note on albinism, including cause and manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 6
**EXP:** Albinism is a group of inherited disorders of melanin synthesis or melanosome biology; the classic biochemical form is tyrosinase deficiency. It causes reduced skin, hair, and ocular pigmentation, photophobia, nystagmus/reduced visual acuity, and increased UV-related skin damage.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q958 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Short note 7): Write a short note on carcinoid tumor (argentaffinoma).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Short note 7
**EXP:** A neuroendocrine tumor, classically of the gut, may secrete serotonin and other vasoactive substances. Systemic carcinoid syndrome can cause flushing, secretory diarrhea, bronchospasm, and right-sided valvular disease; urinary 5-HIAA is a useful marker. Tryptophan diversion can contribute to niacin deficiency.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q972 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Case 5d): Explain the dermatitis and persistent diarrhea in the patient with carcinoid syndrome.

### Q975 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Case 6c): Name the organs involved in creatine synthesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Case 6c
**EXP:** The first step forming guanidinoacetate occurs mainly in kidney (also pancreas); methylation to creatine occurs mainly in liver. Creatine is then taken up by muscle and other tissues.

### Q976 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Case 6d): State the function and importance of creatinine.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Case 6d
**EXP:** Creatinine is the spontaneous breakdown product of creatine/phosphocreatine. It is excreted by the kidneys and used clinically as a marker for glomerular filtration; creatine/phosphocreatine itself buffers and transfers high-energy phosphate in muscle.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q979 (Amino Acid Metabolism, written source pp. 65–67, Case 7c): Explain the causes of intellectual disability and hypopigmentation in the child with PKU.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 65–67, Case 7c
**EXP:** High phenylalanine and phenylketones disrupt brain amino-acid transport and neurotransmitter/developmental pathways. Low tyrosine reduces melanin synthesis, causing hypopigmentation.
**Source Pages:** 65–67
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

Choices: (a) subacute combined degeneration of spinal cord; (b) beriberi; (c) cheilosis; (d) rickets; (e) pellagra; (f) neural-tube defects; (g) pellagra-like manifestations; (h) night blindness; (i) fatty liver; (j) scurvy.

### Q986 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Enumerate 6): List biochemical changes in rickets.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Enumerate 6
**EXP:** Defective mineralization at growth plates, elevated alkaline phosphatase, low phosphate, and low or low-normal calcium; secondary hyperparathyroidism increases phosphate loss. In nutritional vitamin D deficiency, 25-hydroxyvitamin D is low.

**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry

### Q1008 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Function 6): Mention the functions of niacin.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

**EXP:** NAD and NADP carry reducing equivalents in energy metabolism, biosynthesis, and antioxidant reactions.
**Source Pages:** 73–75

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1009 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Function 7): Mention the functions of vitamin K.

**Correct Answer:** -

**EXP:** It is a cofactor for γ-carboxylation of glutamate residues in coagulation factors II, VII, IX, and X and proteins C and S, as well as selected bone proteins.

**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1010 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Function 8): Mention the functions of vitamin D.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Function 8
**EXP:** It maintains calcium and phosphate homeostasis by increasing intestinal absorption, supporting renal handling, and regulating bone mineralization and remodeling.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

**EXP:** Vitamin C deficiency.

### Q1015 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Disease 5): Name the vitamin deficiency that causes night blindness.

**Correct Answer:** -

**EXP:** Vitamin A deficiency.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1016 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Disease 6): Name the vitamin deficiency that causes pellagra.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Disease 6
**EXP:** Niacin (vitamin B3) deficiency; tryptophan deficiency or diversion can also contribute.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1017 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Disease 7): Name the vitamin deficiency associated with hyperkeratosis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Disease 7
**EXP:** Vitamin A deficiency can cause follicular hyperkeratosis. The finding is not specific and may also have other nutritional or dermatologic causes.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1018 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Disease 8): Name the vitamin deficiency that can cause impaired blood clotting.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Disease 8
**EXP:** Vitamin K deficiency.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1019 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 1): On a biochemical basis, explain why vitamin A deficiency prolongs dark adaptation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 1
**EXP:** 11-cis-retinal, derived from vitamin A, combines with opsin to form rhodopsin in rod cells. Deficiency slows rhodopsin regeneration after exposure to light, impairing scotopic vision and prolonging dark adaptation.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1020 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 2): Explain why thiamine deficiency causes neurologic manifestations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 2
**EXP:** Thiamine pyrophosphate is required for pyruvate dehydrogenase, α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, branched-chain α-ketoacid dehydrogenase, and transketolase. Reduced ATP production and altered carbohydrate metabolism particularly injure energy-dependent nervous tissue.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1021 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 3): Explain how PLP and vitamin B12 deficiency can cause anemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 3
**EXP:** PLP is required by ALA synthase in heme synthesis; deficiency can cause impaired heme production and sideroblastic, often microcytic, anemia. B12 deficiency impairs methionine synthase and DNA synthesis, causing megaloblastic anemia.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1022 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 4): Explain why PLP deficiency can cause peripheral neuritis and convulsions.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 4
**EXP:** PLP is needed for synthesis of neurotransmitters, including GABA from glutamate. Reduced inhibitory GABA can cause seizures, while impaired neurotransmitter and sphingolipid metabolism can contribute to neuropathy.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1023 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 5): Explain the neurologic manifestations of vitamin B12 deficiency.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 5
**EXP:** Impaired methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase reactions increase methylmalonic acid and disrupt myelin maintenance. Demyelination may affect posterior and lateral spinal columns and peripheral nerves, causing paresthesia, ataxia, and loss of vibration/position sense.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1024 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 6): Explain causes of anemia in vitamin C deficiency.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 6
**EXP:** Poor collagen support makes capillaries fragile and causes chronic blood loss; vitamin C deficiency can also reduce non-heme iron absorption. These effects may produce iron-deficiency anemia.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1025 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 7): Explain why newborn infants are at risk of vitamin K deficiency.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 7
**EXP:** Placental transfer of vitamin K is limited, newborn stores are low, and the intestine initially lacks vitamin-K-producing bacteria; breast milk contains relatively little. Without prophylaxis, clotting-factor γ-carboxylation may be inadequate.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1026 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 8): Explain why vitamin B6 deficiency may lead to convulsions.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 8
**EXP:** PLP-dependent glutamate decarboxylase makes GABA. Low PLP can reduce GABA synthesis and inhibitory neurotransmission, lowering the seizure threshold.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1027 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 9): Explain why vitamin C is required for normal collagen synthesis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 9
**EXP:** Vitamin C maintains iron in the reduced state for prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases. Hydroxylation stabilizes the collagen triple helix and supports cross-linking and connective-tissue strength.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1028 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Explain 10): Explain why intracranial hemorrhage can occur in newborn infants with vitamin K deficiency.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Explain 10
**EXP:** Insufficient vitamin K prevents γ-carboxylation of clotting factors II, VII, IX, and X and proteins C/S. Impaired coagulation can cause vitamin-K-deficiency bleeding, including intracranial hemorrhage; routine neonatal prophylaxis greatly reduces this risk.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1029 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 1a): A 55-year-old man has progressive night blindness, prior small-intestinal and colonic resection, steatohepatitis, and cirrhotic-appearing liver. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 1a
**EXP:** Vitamin A deficiency causing impaired dark adaptation, likely related to fat malabsorption and impaired hepatic handling/storage.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1030 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 1b): In the patient with night blindness, what are possible causes of the disease?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 1b
**EXP:** Reduced dietary vitamin A, fat malabsorption after intestinal resection, cholestatic or pancreatic disease, and impaired hepatic storage from chronic liver disease are plausible causes in this case.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1031 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 1c): What is the possible treatment for the patient with vitamin A deficiency?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 1c
**EXP:** Replace vitamin A and address the underlying malabsorption/liver disease; use a route and dose appropriate to severity and absorption, with monitoring because excessive vitamin A is toxic.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1032 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 2a): A 45-year-old construction worker has confusion, inappropriate affect, scaly pigmented dermatitis on sun-exposed skin, and severe diarrhea. What is the diagnosis?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 2a
**EXP:** Pellagra due to niacin deficiency, with the classic dermatitis, diarrhea, and neuropsychiatric manifestations.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1033 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 2b): What are possible causes of pellagra in the described patient?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 2b
**EXP:** Poor niacin or tryptophan intake, alcohol-associated malnutrition, malabsorption, Hartnup disease, isoniazid exposure, or diversion of tryptophan to serotonin (including carcinoid syndrome).
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1034 (Vitamins, written source pp. 73–75, Case 2c): What is the possible treatment for the patient with pellagra?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 73–75, Case 2c
**EXP:** Give nicotinamide (or niacin) replacement and correct protein/tryptophan and overall nutritional deficiency; identify and treat the underlying cause.
**Source Pages:** 73–75
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

## Written prompts recovered from DNA-replication pages 85–86

### Q1035 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Match 1): Match each eukaryotic DNA polymerase with its function: (1) DNA polymerase α–primase; (2) DNA polymerase β; (3) DNA polymerase γ; (4) DNA polymerase δ. Choices: (A) synthesis of the lagging strand; (B) synthesis of the mitochondrial genome; (C) DNA repair; (D) synthesis of RNA primers.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Match 1
**EXP:** 1-D, 2-C, 3-B, 4-A, matching the printed source key.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1036 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Enumerate 1): List the eukaryotic DNA polymerases involved in replication and state their functions.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** DNA polymerase α–primase makes the RNA–DNA primer; polymerase ε primarily synthesizes the leading strand; polymerase δ primarily synthesizes the lagging strand and fills/extends Okazaki fragments; polymerase γ replicates mitochondrial DNA. Polymerase β mainly functions in DNA repair.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1037 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Enumerate 2): List the prokaryotic DNA polymerases and state their functions.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** DNA polymerase III performs most leading- and lagging-strand synthesis; DNA polymerase I removes RNA primers with its 5′→3′ exonuclease and fills the resulting gaps; DNA polymerase II functions mainly in repair/restart. Specialized polymerases IV and V perform translesion synthesis in many bacteria.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1038 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Enumerate 3): State the functions of single-strand binding proteins, DnaA, DnaB, and DnaC.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Single-strand binding proteins stabilize unwound DNA and prevent reannealing or secondary structures. DnaA recognizes the bacterial origin and initiates opening; DnaB is the replicative helicase; DnaC loads DnaB onto the origin.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1039 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 1): Explain the importance of telomerase in DNA replication.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 1
**EXP:** Telomerase carries an RNA template and extends chromosome 3′ telomeric ends, allowing completion of lagging-strand replication and limiting progressive shortening of linear chromosomes.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1040 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 2): Explain the statement that cancer cells do not age.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 2
**EXP:** Many cancer cells reactivate telomerase or alternative telomere-lengthening pathways, maintaining chromosome ends and enabling repeated division. This contributes to replicative immortality but is not the only feature of cancer.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1041 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 3): Explain the protective role of telomeres in chromosomes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 3
**EXP:** Telomeric repeats and shelterin proteins cap chromosome ends, distinguishing them from DNA breaks, limiting degradation and end-to-end fusion, and buffering loss of terminal DNA during replication.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1042 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 4): Explain why topoisomerases play an important role in DNA replication.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 4
**EXP:** Unwinding by helicase creates torsional strain and positive supercoils ahead of the fork. Topoisomerases transiently cut and reseal DNA to relieve this strain; type II enzymes can also separate interlinked daughter molecules.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1043 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 5): Explain why telomeres are a target for cancer chemotherapy.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 5
**EXP:** Many tumors rely on telomerase to maintain telomeres and sustain proliferation. Inhibiting telomerase can cause progressive telomere shortening and limit tumor-cell replication, although normal stem cells also use telomerase and clinical targeting is challenging.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1044 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Explain 6): Explain why DNA replication is semiconservative.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Explain 6
**EXP:** Each parental strand serves as a template for synthesis of a complementary new strand. Each daughter DNA duplex therefore contains one conserved parental strand and one newly synthesized strand.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1045 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Compare 1): Compare DNA polymerases of replication in prokaryotes and eukaryotes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Compare 1
**EXP:** Bacteria use DNA polymerase III for most leading/lagging synthesis and DNA polymerase I for primer removal and gap filling; polymerase II and specialized polymerases participate in repair. Eukaryotes use α–primase to initiate, ε mainly for leading-strand synthesis, δ mainly for lagging-strand synthesis, and γ for mitochondrial DNA; β functions chiefly in repair. Eukaryotic replication uses multiple origins and more polymerase/accessory proteins.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1046 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Compare 2): Compare type I and type II topoisomerases.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Compare 2
**EXP:** Type I topoisomerase transiently cuts one DNA strand and generally changes linking number by one without ATP-driven passage of a second duplex. Type II cuts both strands, passes another duplex through the break, changes linking number by two, usually uses ATP, and can decatenate daughter DNA.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1047 (Replication, written source pp. 85–86, Compare 3): Compare the functions of DNA polymerase β and DNA polymerase ε in eukaryotic cells.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 85–86, Compare 3
**EXP:** DNA polymerase β functions mainly in base-excision DNA repair and gap filling. DNA polymerase ε is a high-fidelity replicative polymerase that primarily synthesizes the leading strand and has proofreading activity.
**Source Pages:** 85–86
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---


## Written prompts recovered from transcription pages 90–91

### Q1048 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Define 1): Define the template strand.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Define 1
**EXP:** The DNA strand read by RNA polymerase in the 3′→5′ direction to synthesize a complementary RNA transcript in the 5′→3′ direction.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1049 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Define 2): Define transcription.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Define 2
**EXP:** Synthesis of RNA from a DNA template by a DNA-dependent RNA polymerase.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1050 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Define 3): Define splicing of mRNA.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Define 3
**EXP:** Removal of introns from a precursor mRNA and joining of exons to produce a mature RNA, usually by the spliceosome.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1051 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Define 4): Define general transcription factors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Define 4
**EXP:** Basal proteins required with eukaryotic RNA polymerase to recognize core promoters, assemble the initiation complex, and begin transcription.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1052 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Enumerate 1): List the eukaryotic RNA polymerases and state their functions.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** RNA polymerase I synthesizes most large rRNAs (18S, 5.8S, and 28S); polymerase II synthesizes mRNA and several regulatory RNAs; polymerase III synthesizes tRNAs, 5S rRNA, and other small RNAs.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1053 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Enumerate 2): List post-transcriptional modifications of mRNA and state the importance of each.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** 5′ capping protects the transcript and supports nuclear export and translation initiation; 3′ polyadenylation promotes stability, export, and translation; splicing removes introns and joins exons; alternative splicing and RNA editing can diversify the transcript/protein output.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1054 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 1): Explain how RNA editing changes mRNA after transcription.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 1
**EXP:** RNA editing enzymatically changes selected nucleotides after the initial transcript is made, for example by insertion, deletion, or chemical conversion. The mature RNA sequence can therefore differ from the DNA-templated sequence and may alter coding or regulatory information.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1055 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 2): Explain why sigma and rho factors have different roles in prokaryotic transcription.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 2
**EXP:** Sigma factor associates with bacterial RNA polymerase and directs promoter recognition and initiation. Rho is an ATP-dependent termination factor that can release RNA polymerase at rho-dependent terminators.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1056 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 3): Explain the role of the sigma subunit/factor in initiation of transcription.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 3
**EXP:** Sigma gives bacterial RNA polymerase specificity for promoter sequences, helps position the enzyme at the start site, and supports local DNA opening; it often dissociates or becomes less tightly associated after initiation.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1057 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 4): Explain why the estimated number of proteins can exceed the number of genes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 4
**EXP:** One gene can produce multiple protein products through alternative promoter use, alternative RNA splicing, RNA editing, and post-translational processing or modification. Thus gene count does not equal protein-form count.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1058 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 5): Explain why TFIIH has bifunctional activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 5
**EXP:** TFIIH contains a helicase/translocase activity that opens promoter DNA and a CDK7 kinase activity that phosphorylates the RNA polymerase II C-terminal domain, helping promoter escape and coordinating transcription with RNA processing.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1059 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Explain 6): Explain why ribonucleases do not readily attack the 5′ end of mature mRNA.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Explain 6
**EXP:** The 7-methylguanosine cap is connected by an unusual 5′–5′ triphosphate linkage and bound by cap-binding proteins. This structure protects the 5′ end from exonucleolytic degradation.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1060 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Compare 1): Compare the importance of mRNA capping with mRNA splicing.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Compare 1
**EXP:** The 5′ cap protects RNA from degradation, supports nuclear export, and recruits translation-initiation machinery. Splicing removes introns and joins exons to create a translatable mature sequence; alternative splicing can generate multiple isoforms.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1061 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Compare 2): Compare sigma and rho factors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Compare 2
**EXP:** Sigma is an initiation specificity factor that directs bacterial RNA polymerase to promoters. Rho is an ATP-dependent termination helicase that releases transcript/polymerase at rho-dependent termination sites.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1062 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Compare 3): Compare prokaryotic and eukaryotic promoters.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Compare 3
**EXP:** Bacterial promoters commonly have −10 and −35 elements recognized through sigma factors and one main RNA polymerase. Eukaryotic promoters may contain TATA, initiator, and other core elements and require general transcription factors, chromatin regulation, and polymerase-specific initiation complexes.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1063 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Compare 4): Compare prokaryotic and eukaryotic RNA polymerases.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Compare 4
**EXP:** Bacteria have one principal multisubunit RNA polymerase whose promoter specificity is directed by sigma factors. Eukaryotes have distinct nuclear polymerases I, II, and III for different RNA classes, with separate general transcription factors and more extensive chromatin-dependent control.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1064 (Transcription, written source pp. 90–91, Compare 5): Compare termination of transcription in prokaryotes and eukaryotes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 90–91, Compare 5
**EXP:** Bacteria use intrinsic hairpin/U-rich termination or rho-dependent termination. Eukaryotic termination is polymerase- and transcript-specific: Pol II transcripts are cleaved at a polyadenylation signal and termination follows downstream, whereas Pol I and Pol III use their own termination mechanisms.
**Source Pages:** 90–91
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Source Pages:** 96–97

**Source Pages:** 96–97

### Q1072 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Define 6): Define a synonym codon.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Define 6
**EXP:** A codon different in sequence from another codon but specifying the same amino acid because the genetic code is degenerate.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1073 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 1): Give four causes of mutations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Replication errors, spontaneous chemical changes such as deamination or oxidation, chemical mutagens, and ionizing or ultraviolet radiation.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1074 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 2): List four characteristics of the genetic code.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** It is triplet, degenerate, non-overlapping, and nearly universal. It is also unambiguous for a given codon and read continuously in a fixed frame.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1075 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 3): List four requirements for translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** An mRNA template, ribosomes, charged tRNAs/amino acids with aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, and initiation/elongation/release factors. ATP and GTP provide energy.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1076 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 4): Give three differences between eukaryotic and prokaryotic translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Eukaryotes use 80S ribosomes versus bacterial 70S; the initiator is Met-tRNAᵢ versus fMet-tRNAᶠᴹᵉᵗ; initiation uses cap-dependent scanning/Kozak context versus Shine–Dalgarno positioning. Eukaryotic transcription and translation are also separated by the nuclear envelope.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1077 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 5): Give four post-translational covalent modifications and one example of each.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 5
**EXP:** Phosphorylation (glycogen phosphorylase); glycosylation (secreted glycoproteins); hydroxylation (collagen proline/lysine); and proteolytic processing/trimming (proinsulin to insulin). Acetylation and methylation are additional examples.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1078 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 6): Give four causes of gene mutation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 6
**EXP:** DNA replication errors, spontaneous base damage (for example deamination), chemical mutagens, and radiation/UV exposure. This duplicates the source’s earlier cause-of-mutation prompt and is retained as a separate occurrence.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1079 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 7): Name two mechanisms that may lead to errors during DNA replication.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 7
**EXP:** Tautomeric base shifts can cause incorrect pairing; polymerase slippage on repetitive sequences can cause insertions or deletions. Failure of proofreading or mismatch repair also allows errors to persist.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1080 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Enumerate 8): Name three effects of point mutation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Enumerate 8
**EXP:** A substitution may be silent, missense, or nonsense; it may also affect splicing or regulatory sequences.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1081 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 1): Explain how a tRNA can recognize more than one codon for a specific amino acid.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 1
**EXP:** Flexible “wobble” pairing at the third codon position allows one anticodon to pair with more than one synonymous codon, often through inosine or noncanonical base pairing.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1082 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 2): Explain why all three major classes of RNA are required for translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 2
**EXP:** mRNA carries the codon sequence; tRNA matches codons to amino acids and delivers the correct substrate; rRNA forms the ribosome’s structural/catalytic core and catalyzes peptide-bond formation.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1083 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 3): Explain why eIF4 plays an important role in translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 3
**EXP:** The eIF4F complex recognizes the 5′ cap through eIF4E, unwinds structured 5′ UTR through eIF4A, and links the mRNA to the 40S subunit through eIF4G, supporting initiation and ribosome scanning.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1084 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 4): Explain the importance of the two tRNA-binding sites on the 80S ribosome.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 4
**EXP:** The A site accepts the incoming aminoacyl-tRNA matched to the next codon. The P site holds the tRNA carrying the growing peptide, positioning substrates for peptide-bond formation and translocation.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1085 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 5): Explain the role of release factors in translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 5
**EXP:** Release factors recognize a stop codon in the A site and promote hydrolysis of the peptidyl-tRNA bond, releasing the completed polypeptide and allowing ribosome recycling.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1086 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 6): Explain why trimming is an important post-translational modification.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 6
**EXP:** Proteolytic removal of signal peptides or propeptides can target proteins to the correct compartment and activate or mature them; examples include insulin maturation and activation of digestive zymogens.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1087 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 7): Explain why covalent modification is an important post-translational process.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 7
**EXP:** Covalent changes such as phosphorylation, glycosylation, acetylation, hydroxylation, and proteolytic cleavage can regulate activity, stability, localization, interactions, and maturation after translation.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1088 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 8): Explain why DNA contains thymine rather than uracil.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 8
**EXP:** Cytosine spontaneously deaminates to uracil. Using thymine as the normal DNA base lets repair systems recognize uracil as damage and restore the correct sequence; RNA is shorter-lived and uses uracil.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1089 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 9): Explain why a base substitution may have no effect or may cause serious effects.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 9
**EXP:** A substitution can be synonymous because of genetic-code degeneracy, or it can change an amino acid, create a stop codon, alter splicing, or disrupt a regulatory site. The effect depends on location and protein context.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1090 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Explain 10): Explain why a deletion mutation may or may not cause a frameshift.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Explain 10
**EXP:** A deletion within a coding region shifts the reading frame if the number of deleted bases is not divisible by three. Deletion of three or a multiple of three removes whole codon(s) without shifting the downstream frame; deletions outside coding sequence may not alter it.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1091 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Compare 1): Compare the effects of a missense mutation and a nonsense mutation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Compare 1
**EXP:** A missense mutation substitutes one amino acid for another and may alter protein function to varying degrees. A nonsense mutation creates a premature stop codon, often producing a truncated or degraded protein.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1092 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Compare 2): Compare eukaryotic and prokaryotic translation.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Compare 2
**EXP:** Eukaryotes use 80S ribosomes, Met initiator tRNA, cap-dependent scanning, and many eukaryotic initiation factors; prokaryotes use 70S ribosomes, fMet initiator tRNA, Shine–Dalgarno positioning, and bacterial factors. In eukaryotes transcription is nuclear and translation cytoplasmic; bacteria can couple the two.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1093 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Compare 3): Compare missense and silent mutations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Compare 3
**EXP:** Missense changes a codon to specify a different amino acid; silent changes the nucleotide sequence but retains the same amino acid. Either may have additional effects if a splice or regulatory sequence is involved.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1094 (Translation, written source pp. 96–97, Compare 4): Compare silent and nonsense mutations.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 96–97, Compare 4
**EXP:** A silent substitution retains the encoded amino acid; a nonsense substitution changes an amino-acid codon to a stop codon and can truncate the protein.
**Source Pages:** 96–97
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1095 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 1): Define chromatin remodeling.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 1
**EXP:** Changes in nucleosome positioning, histone composition, or histone chemical modifications that alter DNA accessibility to transcription and other DNA-binding proteins.

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1096 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 2): Define cis-acting elements.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 2
**EXP:** Regulatory DNA sequences that affect expression of genes on the same DNA molecule, such as promoters, enhancers, silencers, and response elements.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1097 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 3): Define trans-acting elements.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 3
**EXP:** Diffusible regulatory molecules, usually proteins or regulatory RNAs, that bind or otherwise act on cis elements to control gene expression.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1098 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 4): Define hormone-response elements.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 4
**EXP:** Specific cis-regulatory DNA sequences recognized by hormone-bound nuclear receptors to regulate transcription of target genes.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1099 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 5): Define tissue-specific elements.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 5
**EXP:** Cis-regulatory DNA sequences whose activity depends on transcription factors or chromatin states present in particular cell or tissue types.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1100 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Define 6): Define enhancers of transcription.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Define 6
**EXP:** Cis-regulatory DNA sequences that bind activator proteins and increase transcription from a promoter, often over long distances and in either orientation through DNA looping.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1101 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Explain 1): Explain the role of chromatin remodeling in regulation of gene expression.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Explain 1
**EXP:** Chromatin remodeling changes how accessible DNA is to transcription factors and RNA polymerase. Nucleosome repositioning and histone modifications such as acetylation can expose promoter/enhancer DNA and activate transcription, while compact chromatin or deacetylation can repress it.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1102 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Explain 2): Explain how ApoB48 and ApoB100 proteins are encoded by the same gene.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Explain 2
**EXP:** In intestinal cells, site-specific RNA editing converts a CAA codon for glutamine into UAA, creating a stop codon and the shorter ApoB48. In liver, the transcript is not edited at that site, so full-length ApoB100 is translated.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1103 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Explain 3): Explain how some cancer patients develop resistance to methotrexate chemotherapy.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Explain 3
**EXP:** A common mechanism is amplification or overexpression of dihydrofolate reductase, the drug target, sometimes with altered drug transport or target affinity. More target enzyme can overcome inhibition and permit folate-dependent nucleotide synthesis.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1104 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Explain 4): Explain why the number of antibodies can exceed the number of immunoglobulin genes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Explain 4
**EXP:** V(D)J segment recombination, junctional diversity, combinatorial pairing of heavy and light chains, and somatic hypermutation create many antibody specificities from a finite gene-segment repertoire.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1105 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Explain 5): Explain the role of microRNA in regulation of gene expression.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Explain 5
**EXP:** Mature miRNAs associate with Argonaute/RISC and base-pair with target mRNAs, commonly in the 3′ UTR. This can inhibit translation and promote deadenylation, decapping, or transcript degradation; one miRNA may regulate many targets.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1106 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Compare 1): Compare cis-acting and trans-acting elements.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Compare 1
**EXP:** Cis elements are DNA regulatory sequences on the same molecule as the regulated gene. Trans factors are diffusible proteins or RNAs that recognize or influence those sequences and can regulate multiple genes.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1107 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Compare 2): Compare basal and regulatory sequences involved in gene expression.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Compare 2
**EXP:** Basal/core promoter sequences position the transcription machinery and support basal initiation near the transcription start site. Regulatory sequences such as enhancers, silencers, and response elements bind specific factors to increase or decrease transcription in context-dependent ways.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1108 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Compare 3): Compare DNA methylation with histone acetylation in regulation of gene expression.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Compare 3
**EXP:** Promoter CpG DNA methylation is generally associated with transcriptional repression through reduced factor binding and recruitment of repressive complexes. Histone acetylation neutralizes lysine positive charge, loosens histone–DNA interaction, and is generally associated with accessible, active chromatin; effects remain context-dependent.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1109 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Compare 4): Compare alternative splicing of mRNA with RNA editing.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Compare 4
**EXP:** Alternative splicing joins different combinations of existing exons from one pre-mRNA to make distinct isoforms. RNA editing changes nucleotide identities or inserts/deletes nucleotides after transcription, altering the RNA sequence itself.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1110 (Regulation of Gene Expression, written source p. 100, Compare 5): Compare the effects of phosphorylation on eIF4 and eIF2.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 100, Compare 5
**EXP:** The source’s “IF4/IF2” refers to eukaryotic initiation factors. Phosphorylation of eIF2α inhibits initiation by trapping eIF2B and lowering active eIF2–GTP–Met-tRNAᵢ. Phosphorylation of eIF4E/eIF4F-associated regulation often promotes cap-dependent initiation, although the effect depends on which eIF4 component and signaling context is meant.
**Source Pages:** 100
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---


## Written prompts recovered from extracellular-matrix page 103

### Q1111 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Explain 1): Explain why collagen molecules have a very firm structure.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Explain 1
**EXP:** Three polypeptide α-chains form a tightly packed triple helix with glycine at every third residue; hydroxyproline/hydroxylysine stabilize the helix, and covalent cross-links between molecules add tensile strength.
**Source Pages:** 103
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1112 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Explain 2): Explain why collagen becomes less flexible with age.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Explain 2
**EXP:** Age-related enzymatic cross-linking and accumulation of nonenzymatic advanced-glycation cross-links between collagen molecules increase stiffness and reduce tissue compliance.
**Source Pages:** 103
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1113 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Explain 3): Explain why elastin is rubber-like.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Explain 3
**EXP:** Elastin chains are highly flexible and hydrophobic and are joined by desmosine/isodesmosine cross-links. The network can stretch and recoil after the force is removed.
**Source Pages:** 103
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1114 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Enumerate 1): Name three connective-tissue proteins.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Collagen, elastin, and fibronectin.
**Source Pages:** 103
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Type I collagen is the principal collagenous bone protein; osteocalcin is a non-collagenous bone matrix protein.

**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry
