### Q1116 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Enumerate 3): Name one collagenous and one non-collagenous cartilage protein.

**EXP:** Type II collagen is the principal collagenous protein; aggrecan is a major non-collagenous proteoglycan.

### Q1117 (Extracellular Matrix, written source p. 103, Enumerate 4): Name three structures or molecules to which fibronectin can bind.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 103, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Fibronectin binds integrins/cell surfaces, collagen, and heparin or heparan-sulfate proteoglycans; it can also bind fibrin.

## Written prompts recovered from muscle fuels, gout, hemoproteins, heme, cholesterol, and lipoprotein pages 104–121

### Q1119 (Fuels of Muscle, written source p. 104, Enumerate 1): List the mechanisms that replenish ATP stores in muscle.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 104, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Phosphocreatine donates phosphate to ADP via creatine kinase; anaerobic glycolysis rapidly produces ATP; mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation supplies sustained ATP; and adenylate kinase converts 2 ADP to ATP + AMP.
**Source Pages:** 104
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1120 (Fuels of Muscle, written source p. 105, Compare 1): Compare marathon runners with sprinters.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 105, Compare 1
**EXP:** Marathon runners rely mainly on oxidative type I fibers, aerobic metabolism, fatty acids and glycogen over prolonged exercise, and hepatic glucose output as glycogen is depleted. Sprinters rely more on type II fibers, stored ATP/phosphocreatine, and rapid anaerobic glycolysis; muscle glycogen is used rapidly and fatigue develops sooner.
**Source Pages:** 105
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1121 (Fuels of Muscle, written source p. 105, Compare 2): Compare slow-twitch and fast-twitch muscle fibers.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 105, Compare 2
**EXP:** Type I slow-twitch fibers are rich in myoglobin, mitochondria, and capillaries, use oxidative metabolism, contract more slowly, and resist fatigue. Type II fast-twitch fibers contract rapidly and powerfully, have lower oxidative capacity and often greater glycolytic capacity, and fatigue more quickly; fiber subtypes vary.
**Source Pages:** 105
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1122 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 107, Enumerate 1): Name three mechanisms/causes of metabolic gout.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 107, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Urate overproduction from increased purine synthesis or cell turnover; reduced renal urate excretion; and a combination of increased production with reduced excretion.
**Source Pages:** 107
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1123 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 107, Enumerate 2): Name three causes of hypouricemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 107, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Xanthine oxidase deficiency, uricosuric drugs such as probenecid, and severe liver disease or renal tubular urate-wasting disorders can lower serum urate.
**Source Pages:** 107
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1124 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 107, Enumerate 3): Name three genetic causes of hyperuricemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 107, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** HGPRT deficiency (Lesch–Nyhan or partial deficiency), PRPP synthetase overactivity, and glycogen-storage disease type I (Von Gierke disease) are inherited causes.
**Source Pages:** 107
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1125 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Explain 1): Explain why allopurinol is used to treat gout.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Explain 1
**EXP:** Allopurinol is a hypoxanthine analog converted to oxypurinol; both inhibit xanthine oxidase, reducing conversion of hypoxanthine and xanthine to uric acid. This lowers urate production and can prevent urate stones.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1126 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Explain 2): Explain how alcohol intake may precipitate an acute attack of gouty arthritis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Explain 2
**EXP:** Alcohol can increase ATP degradation and purine/urate production, while lactate and ketone production and dehydration reduce renal urate clearance. The rise in urate can promote monosodium-urate crystal precipitation and an inflammatory flare.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1127 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Explain 3): Explain why NSAIDs such as indomethacin and corticosteroids are used to treat gout.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Explain 3
**EXP:** Acute gout is an inflammatory response to monosodium-urate crystals. NSAIDs inhibit cyclooxygenase-derived prostaglandins, while corticosteroids suppress inflammatory gene expression and cytokines; they relieve the flare but do not lower urate stores.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1128 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Case 1): A 30-year-old woman with leukemia has severe foot-joint pain, renal colic, high serum uric acid, and urinary urate crystals. What caused her hyperuricemia?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Case 1
**EXP:** High turnover and lysis of leukemic cells release nucleic acids; purine catabolism increases uric-acid production, causing hyperuricemia, urate crystalluria, and gout.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1129 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Case 2): For the leukemic patient with gout, give four other causes of hyperuricemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Case 2
**EXP:** Reduced renal excretion/chronic kidney disease, Lesch–Nyhan syndrome or partial HGPRT deficiency, PRPP synthetase overactivity, and Von Gierke disease are examples. Diuretics, alcohol, and tumor lysis are additional causes.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1130 (Biochemical Basis of Gout, written source p. 108, Case 3): For the patient with hyperuricemia, name a drug used to lower urate and explain its action.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 108, Case 3
**EXP:** Allopurinol (or febuxostat) inhibits xanthine oxidase and decreases uric-acid formation. Allopurinol is a standard source-compatible answer for chronic urate lowering; acute pain requires separate anti-inflammatory treatment.
**Source Pages:** 108
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1131 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Compare 1): Compare HbA with fetal hemoglobin (HbF).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Compare 1
**EXP:** HbA is α₂β₂ and predominates after the postnatal switch; HbF is α₂γ₂. HbF binds 2,3-BPG less strongly and therefore has higher oxygen affinity, facilitating transfer of oxygen from maternal blood.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1132 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Compare 2): Compare hemoglobin with myoglobin in structure and function.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Compare 2
**EXP:** Hemoglobin is a tetramer in red cells with cooperative, regulated oxygen binding and transport between lungs and tissues. Myoglobin is a monomeric, single-heme muscle protein with higher oxygen affinity, serving as an oxygen store and facilitating diffusion.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1133 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Compare 3): Compare HbS with HbC.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Compare 3
**EXP:** HbS results from β6 Glu→Val and polymerizes when deoxygenated, causing sickling and hemolysis. HbC results from β6 Glu→Lys and tends to crystallize in red cells, causing a generally milder hemolytic anemia.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1134 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Explain 1): Explain why apomyoglobin reduces heme affinity for carbon monoxide.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Explain 1
**EXP:** The globin pocket sterically constrains heme ligand binding. Its distal histidine stabilizes bound oxygen by hydrogen bonding but makes linear CO binding less favorable, greatly reducing CO affinity compared with free heme.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1135 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Explain 2): Explain the importance of the glutamate residue at position 6 in the β-globin chain.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Explain 2
**EXP:** The negatively charged, hydrophilic β6 glutamate keeps deoxyhemoglobin soluble. Its replacement by hydrophobic valine in HbS creates a hydrophobic patch that promotes polymerization of deoxygenated hemoglobin and red-cell sickling.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1136 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Explain 3): Explain why the globin part of hemoglobin is essential for normal hemoglobin function.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Explain 3
**EXP:** Globin holds and protects heme, provides the heme pocket that tunes oxygen affinity and reduces inappropriate carbon-monoxide binding, and in the tetramer enables cooperative oxygen binding and allosteric regulation.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1137 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Explain 4): Explain why a fetus may appear normal at birth in β-thalassemia major.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Explain 4
**EXP:** Fetal hemoglobin contains γ rather than β chains (α₂γ₂), so severe β-chain deficiency may be masked before birth. Symptoms emerge as γ-chain production falls and HbA production should replace HbF after birth.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1138 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Enumerate 1): Name three minor forms of normal hemoglobin.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** HbA₂ (α₂δ₂), HbF (α₂γ₂), and glycated HbA1c are commonly listed minor hemoglobin forms; HbA1c is a post-translationally glycated fraction rather than a separate globin-chain tetramer.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1139 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Enumerate 2): Name three causes of methemoglobinemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Oxidant drugs or chemicals (for example nitrates or dapsone), cytochrome b₅ reductase deficiency, and hemoglobin M variants.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1140 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Enumerate 3): Name three hemoglobinopathies.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Sickle-cell disease (HbS), HbC disease, and thalassemias.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1141 (Chemistry of Hemoproteins, written source p. 111, Enumerate 4): Name four hemoproteins and state a function of each.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 111, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Hemoglobin transports oxygen and carbon dioxide; myoglobin stores/facilitates oxygen in muscle; cytochromes transfer electrons; catalase decomposes hydrogen peroxide. Peroxidases are another valid example.
**Source Pages:** 111
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1142 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 114, Enumerate 1): Name three causes of unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 114, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Increased heme breakdown/hemolysis, impaired hepatic uptake or conjugation (including neonatal immaturity, Gilbert, or Crigler–Najjar syndromes), and resorption of a large hematoma.
**Source Pages:** 114
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1143 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 114, Enumerate 2): Name three causes of hemolytic jaundice.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 114, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Sickle-cell disease or other hemolytic anemia, G6PD-related hemolysis, and malaria are examples; thalassemia and transfusion reactions are also causes.
**Source Pages:** 114
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1144 (Heme Metabolism, written source p. 114, Enumerate 3): Name two causes of conjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 114, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Biliary obstruction/cholestasis and hepatocellular excretory disease such as hepatitis; inherited defects such as Dubin–Johnson or Rotor syndrome are additional causes.
**Source Pages:** 114
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

**EXP:** It is a component of cell membranes, precursor of steroid hormones, precursor of bile acids/bile salts, and precursor of vitamin D.

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

**EXP:** Hyperthyroidism, severe liver disease, and malnutrition/malabsorption are causes. Abetalipoproteinemia is an inherited example.

### Q1149 (Cholesterol Metabolism, written source p. 118, Mention 1): Name the precursor of cholesterol.

**Source Pages:** 118
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1151 (Cholesterol Metabolism, written source p. 118, Mention 3): State a normal fasting plasma level of total cholesterol.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 118, Mention 3
**EXP:** A desirable adult total cholesterol is generally <200 mg/dL; the precise reference and risk interpretation depend on the laboratory and clinical context.
**Source Pages:** 118
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1152 (Cholesterol Metabolism, written source p. 119, Explain 1): Explain why hyperthyroidism may lead to hypocholesterolemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 119, Explain 1
**EXP:** Thyroid hormone increases hepatic LDL-receptor expression and LDL clearance and can promote conversion of cholesterol to bile acids, lowering circulating cholesterol.
**Source Pages:** 119
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1153 (Cholesterol Metabolism, written source p. 119, Explain 2): Explain why obstructive jaundice may lead to hypercholesterolemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 119, Explain 2
**EXP:** Biliary obstruction impairs biliary cholesterol and bile-acid excretion. Cholesterol and lipoprotein-X can accumulate in plasma, raising measured cholesterol.
**Source Pages:** 119
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1154 (Cholesterol Metabolism, written source p. 119, Explain 3): Explain why diabetes mellitus may be associated with hypercholesterolemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 119, Explain 3
**EXP:** Insulin deficiency/resistance increases adipose lipolysis and hepatic fatty-acid delivery, promotes VLDL production, and may reduce lipoprotein-lipase-mediated clearance. The resulting diabetic dyslipidemia commonly includes high triglycerides and may raise total/non-HDL cholesterol.
**Source Pages:** 119
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1155 (Plasma Lipids and Lipoproteins, written source p. 121, Enumerate 1): Name two cofactors important for lipoprotein lipase activity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 121, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Apolipoprotein C-II is the essential classic activator; ApoA-V facilitates lipoprotein-lipase-mediated triglyceride clearance. Heparin releases endothelial-bound enzyme into plasma but is not itself the catalytic cofactor.
**Source Pages:** 121
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**EXP:** ApoA-I and ApoA-II.
**Source Pages:** 121
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1157 (Plasma Lipids and Lipoproteins, written source p. 121, Enumerate 3): List four functions of apolipoproteins.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 121, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** They provide structural support for lipoprotein particles, serve as enzyme cofactors/activators (ApoC-II for LPL, ApoA-I for LCAT), act as receptor ligands (ApoB-100 and ApoE), and regulate particle assembly, metabolism, and tissue delivery.
**Source Pages:** 121
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

**EXP:** Familial chylomicronemia (LPL/ApoC-II pathway), familial hypercholesterolemia (LDLR/APOB/PCSK9), familial combined hyperlipidemia, and familial dysbetalipoproteinemia (ApoE2) are examples.

**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q1159 (Plasma Lipids and Lipoproteins, written source p. 121, Enumerate 5): Name two causes of hypolipoproteinemia.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q1161 (Plasma Lipids and Lipoproteins, written source p. 121, Enumerate 7): Name four risk factors for atherosclerosis.

**Correct Answer:** -

## Written prompts recovered from diabetes pages 128–130

### Q1162 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 1): Name two complications of diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Diabetic retinopathy and diabetic nephropathy are microvascular complications. Other valid examples include neuropathy, coronary artery disease, stroke, and peripheral arterial disease.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1163 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 2): Name two common precipitants of diabetic ketoacidosis or hyperosmolar coma.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Infection and omission/interruption of insulin or diabetes medication are common precipitants. Myocardial infarction, stroke, glucocorticoids, and dehydration are other triggers.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1164 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 3): Name two changes in lipid metabolism in diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Increased adipose lipolysis and plasma free fatty acids, with increased hepatic VLDL/triglyceride production and reduced clearance; severe insulin deficiency can also promote ketogenesis.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1165 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 4): Name two changes in carbohydrate metabolism in diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Increased hepatic gluconeogenesis/glycogenolysis and reduced insulin-dependent glucose uptake/glycogenesis produce hyperglycemia; glucose may spill into urine when the renal threshold is exceeded.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1166 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 5): State the ADA criteria for diagnosis of diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 5
**EXP:** Any one of: A1C ≥6.5%; fasting plasma glucose ≥126 mg/dL after at least 8 h fasting; 2-h plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL during a 75-g OGTT; or random plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL with classic hyperglycemic symptoms or hyperglycemic crisis. Without unequivocal hyperglycemia, confirm with two abnormal results. The source’s short prompt is updated to the current criteria.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1167 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 6): Name two conditions in which fructosamine is more suitable than glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c).

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 6
**EXP:** Conditions that distort red-cell lifespan or HbA1c interpretation—such as hemolytic anemia and recent blood loss/transfusion—can make fructosamine useful for shorter-term monitoring, provided serum albumin turnover is not substantially abnormal.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1168 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 7): Name three causes of insulin resistance.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 7
**EXP:** Visceral obesity, physical inactivity, and genetic/familial susceptibility. Endocrine disorders and medications such as glucocorticoids can also contribute.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1169 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 8): Name two conditions associated with a flat oral glucose-tolerance-test curve.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 8
**EXP:** Intestinal malabsorption and endocrine deficiency such as adrenal or pituitary insufficiency are reported causes; hypothyroidism and rapid glucose disposal are other possibilities. A flat curve is nonspecific and is not by itself diagnostic of diabetes or organic hypoglycemia.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1170 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Enumerate 9): Name two causes of a lag (steeple) curve on an oral glucose-tolerance test.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Enumerate 9
**EXP:** Rapid gastric emptying after gastrectomy and delayed insulin response/early glucose intolerance can produce a high intermediate peak with a later return toward normal; this historical curve pattern is nonspecific.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1171 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 1): State the treatment of hypoglycemic coma.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 1
**EXP:** Give rapid glucose, preferably intravenous dextrose when access is available; give glucagon when IV access is not immediately available. Identify and treat the cause and monitor for recurrence.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1172 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 2): Name a drug that decreases insulin secretion.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 2
**EXP:** Diazoxide inhibits insulin release by opening β-cell ATP-sensitive potassium channels.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1173 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 3): Name a drug that decreases intestinal carbohydrate absorption.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 3
**EXP:** Acarbose inhibits intestinal brush-border α-glucosidases and delays carbohydrate digestion/absorption.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry

### Q1174 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 4): Name a drug that improves insulin sensitivity.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 4
**EXP:** Metformin improves insulin sensitivity and reduces hepatic glucose production; thiazolidinediones are another class.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 5
**EXP:** An SGLT2 inhibitor, such as empagliflozin or dapagliflozin.
**Source Pages:** 128–130

### Q1176 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 6): State the normal fasting plasma glucose level.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 6
**EXP:** A typical normal fasting plasma glucose is <100 mg/dL (with fasting defined as at least 8 hours without calories).
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None

### Q1177 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 7): State a normal 2-hour plasma glucose after a standard oral glucose load.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 7
**EXP:** A 2-h plasma glucose <140 mg/dL after a 75-g OGTT is normal; the source says “post-prandial,” so the standardized test condition is specified here.
**Source Pages:** 128–130

### Q1178 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Mention 8): State the HbA1c value diagnostic of diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Mention 8
**EXP:** HbA1c ≥6.5% (48 mmol/mol), measured with a standardized laboratory method. Conditions that alter red-cell turnover can make A1c unreliable.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1179 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Compare 1): Compare type 1 with type 2 diabetes mellitus.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Compare 1
**EXP:** Type 1 diabetes results from β-cell destruction and absolute insulin deficiency, often autoimmune, and requires insulin; ketosis is more common. Type 2 combines insulin resistance with progressive relative β-cell failure; it is often associated with obesity and initially managed with lifestyle and non-insulin agents, although insulin may be needed. Either type can occur at any age.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1180 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Compare 2): Compare hyperglycemic ketoacidotic coma, hyperosmolar coma, and hypoglycemic coma.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Compare 2
**EXP:** DKA features insulin deficiency, hyperglycemia, ketonemia, and metabolic acidosis, often developing over hours. HHS features profound hyperglycemia, hyperosmolality, and dehydration with little or no significant ketosis, often in type 2 diabetes. Hypoglycemic coma is due to dangerously low glucose, often from insulin or medication, and can develop rapidly. All are emergencies but require different treatment.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1181 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 1): Explain the role of Ca²⁺ in insulin secretion.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 1
**EXP:** Glucose metabolism raises ATP, closing β-cell KATP channels and depolarizing the membrane. Voltage-gated Ca²⁺ channels open; Ca²⁺ entry triggers exocytosis of insulin granules.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1182 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 2): Explain how vitamin D supplementation may improve type 2 diabetes biochemically.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 2
**EXP:** Vitamin D receptors and vitamin-D-dependent calcium regulation may support β-cell function and insulin secretion; vitamin D status may also influence insulin sensitivity. Any glycemic benefit is context-dependent, and supplementation is not a replacement for established diabetes treatment.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1183 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 3): Explain how hyperglycemia causes vascular complications.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 3
**EXP:** Chronic hyperglycemia promotes advanced glycation end products, oxidative stress, protein kinase C and polyol-pathway changes, endothelial dysfunction, basement-membrane injury, and inflammation. These mechanisms damage small and large vessels.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1184 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 4): Explain why oral hypoglycemic drugs act through different mechanisms.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 4
**EXP:** Type 2 diabetes has several defects, so drug classes target different steps: metformin reduces hepatic glucose output; sulfonylureas stimulate β-cell insulin release; α-glucosidase inhibitors delay intestinal carbohydrate digestion; thiazolidinediones improve insulin sensitivity; and SGLT2 inhibitors increase urinary glucose loss.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1185 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 5): Explain insulin catabolism.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 5
**EXP:** Insulin binds its receptor and is internalized; intracellular insulin-degrading enzyme and proteases degrade it. Liver and kidney are major sites of insulin clearance, with renal filtration and tubular degradation contributing.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1186 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 6): Explain the mechanism of insulin action.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 6
**EXP:** Insulin binds the extracellular α subunits of its tetrameric receptor, activating β-subunit tyrosine kinase and receptor autophosphorylation. IRS–PI3K–Akt signaling promotes GLUT4 translocation, glycogen/lipid/protein synthesis, and suppression of hepatic glucose output; MAPK signaling also regulates growth and gene expression.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1187 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Explain 7): Explain why glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c) is useful for follow-up of diabetes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Explain 7
**EXP:** HbA1c forms by nonenzymatic glycation over the red-cell lifespan and reflects average glycemia over roughly the preceding 2–3 months, with greater weighting of recent weeks. It is less affected by a single meal or daily fluctuation, but is unreliable when red-cell turnover or hemoglobin characteristics are altered.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1188 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 1a): An obese 50-year-old woman has polydipsia, polyuria, glucosuria, and random glucose 320 mg/dL. What is the most probable diagnosis?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 1a
**EXP:** Diabetes mellitus, most likely type 2 given the age and obesity. Classic symptoms plus random plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL meet diagnostic criteria.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1189 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 1b): For the obese woman with newly identified diabetes, what is the likely etiology?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 1b
**EXP:** Type 2 diabetes due to insulin resistance with progressive relative β-cell dysfunction; obesity is a major contributing risk factor.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1190 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 1c): Would you expect increased vascular complications in the woman with diabetes? Explain.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 1c
**EXP:** Yes, persistent hyperglycemia increases microvascular and macrovascular risk through glycation, oxidative stress, and endothelial injury. Risk is reduced by sustained glycemic and cardiovascular risk-factor control.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1191 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 2a): A 16-year-old boy has polyuria, polydipsia, 6-kg weight loss, fasting glucose 280 mg/dL, postprandial glucose 380 mg/dL, and HbA1c 10.5%. What is the most probable diagnosis?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 2a
**EXP:** Diabetes mellitus, most likely type 1 in this symptomatic adolescent with weight loss; type should be confirmed clinically and with appropriate testing.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None

**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

### Q1192 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 2b): Comment on the 16-year-old boy’s HbA1c of 10.5%.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 2b
**EXP:** It is markedly elevated and indicates substantial chronic hyperglycemia over the preceding several months, not just a transient glucose rise.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1193 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 2c): What is the importance of measuring HbA1c in the boy with diabetes?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 2c
**EXP:** It estimates longer-term glycemic exposure and supports monitoring of treatment response and complication risk. It should be interpreted cautiously if red-cell turnover or hemoglobin is abnormal.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1194 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 2d): What is suitable treatment for the 16-year-old boy with likely type 1 diabetes?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 2d
**EXP:** Insulin replacement is required, along with diabetes education, glucose monitoring, nutrition guidance, and screening for acute metabolic complications.
**Source Pages:** 128–130
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1195 (Biochemistry of Diabetes, written source pp. 128–130, Case 2e): Mention two vascular complications that may occur in the boy with diabetes.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** pp. 128–130, Case 2e
**EXP:** Diabetic retinopathy and nephropathy are microvascular complications. Peripheral neuropathy and coronary/peripheral arterial disease are also possible.
**Source Pages:** 128–130

## Written prompts recovered from digestion and absorption page 133

### Q1196 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 1): Name the enzyme responsible for lipid digestion in adults.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Pancreatic lipase is the major digestive lipase in adults.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1197 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 2): Name the enzyme responsible for lipid digestion in children.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Gastric lipase has greater relative importance in infants/young children because pancreatic lipase secretion and bile-acid handling are immature; pancreatic lipase still contributes as the system matures.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1198 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 3): In what form do digested lipids enter intestinal mucosal cells?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Products of lipid digestion—long-chain fatty acids, 2-monoacylglycerol, cholesterol, and other lipids—are delivered to the brush border in mixed micelles and diffuse/are transported into enterocytes. Bile salts generally remain in the lumen.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1199 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 4): In what form are absorbed dietary lipids transported into the circulation?

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 4
**EXP:** Long-chain lipids are re-esterified to triacylglycerol and packaged into chylomicrons, which enter lymph and then blood. Short- and medium-chain fatty acids can enter portal blood bound to albumin.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1200 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 5): Name two endopeptidases.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 5
**EXP:** Trypsin and chymotrypsin. Pepsin is another endopeptidase.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1201 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 6): Name two exopeptidases.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 6
**EXP:** Carboxypeptidase and aminopeptidase; dipeptidases also release terminal amino acids from small peptides.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1202 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 7): Name two causes of steatorrhea.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 7
**EXP:** Pancreatic exocrine insufficiency and reduced bile delivery from cholestasis/biliary obstruction. Small-intestinal mucosal disease is another cause.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1203 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Enumerate 8): Name two mechanisms for absorption of L-amino acids.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Enumerate 8
**EXP:** Most free amino acids use carrier-mediated secondary active transport, commonly Na⁺-dependent cotransport across the apical membrane; other amino-acid-specific carrier systems operate at the apical and basolateral membranes. Small peptides also enter via H⁺-coupled PepT1 and are hydrolyzed intracellularly.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1204 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Explain 1): Explain why gastric lipase is more significant in infants than adults.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Explain 1
**EXP:** Infants have relatively immature pancreatic exocrine secretion and bile-acid handling. Acid-stable gastric lipase therefore contributes more to initial fat digestion, alongside lingual lipase; its relative contribution declines as pancreatic digestion matures.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1205 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Explain 2): Explain why lactose intolerance produces vomiting, watery diarrhea, and colic after lactose ingestion.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Explain 2
**EXP:** Lactase deficiency leaves lactose unhydrolyzed and unabsorbed. Luminal lactose retains water osmotically and is fermented by bacteria to acids and gas, causing watery diarrhea, distension, cramps, and sometimes nausea/vomiting.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1206 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Explain 3): Explain why lipid emulsification is important for pancreatic-lipase action.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Explain 3
**EXP:** Bile salts emulsify large lipid globules into smaller droplets, increasing the lipid–water interface available to pancreatic lipase. Colipase anchors lipase at the droplet surface despite bile salts.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1207 (Biochemistry of Digestion and Absorption, written source p. 133, Explain 4): Explain why cellulose is an important dietary component although it is non-digestible by humans.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 133, Explain 4
**EXP:** Humans lack cellulase, so cellulose is dietary fiber. It increases stool bulk and water retention, supports bowel regularity, and can be partly fermented by colonic microbes.
**Source Pages:** 133
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---


## Written prompts recovered from fatty-liver and acid-base pages 135 and 138

### Q1208 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Enumerate 1): Name three vitamins whose deficiency may cause fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Enumerate 1
**EXP:** Deficiency of choline and other methyl-donor nutrients can impair hepatic lipoprotein export; folate and vitamin B₁₂ deficiencies can contribute through impaired one-carbon metabolism. The source’s use of “vitamins” is broad; choline is an essential nutrient rather than a conventional vitamin.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1209 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Enumerate 2): Name five lipotropic factors.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Enumerate 2
**EXP:** Choline, methionine, betaine, folate, and vitamin B₁₂ are classic lipotropic factors/nutrients that support phosphatidylcholine synthesis or methyl transfer and hepatic lipid export.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1210 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Enumerate 3): Name three causes of fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Enumerate 3
**EXP:** Increased fatty-acid influx from adipose tissue, reduced hepatic β-oxidation (for example with alcohol), and impaired triacylglycerol export due to deficient apoprotein/phospholipid synthesis. Excess de novo lipogenesis is another cause.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1211 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Explain 1): Explain how overfeeding carbohydrate can cause fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Explain 1
**EXP:** Excess carbohydrate increases glycolytic acetyl-CoA and NADPH supply for de novo fatty-acid synthesis. Fatty acids are esterified to triacylglycerol; if hepatic oxidation or VLDL export cannot keep pace, triacylglycerol accumulates in the liver.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1212 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Explain 2): Explain how chronic alcoholism can cause fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Explain 2
**EXP:** Alcohol oxidation raises the hepatic NADH/NAD⁺ ratio, suppressing fatty-acid β-oxidation and favoring triacylglycerol synthesis. Alcohol can also increase fatty-acid delivery and impair lipoprotein export, promoting hepatic fat accumulation.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1213 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Explain 3): Explain how diabetes mellitus can cause fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Explain 3
**EXP:** Insulin deficiency/resistance increases adipose lipolysis and delivery of free fatty acids to liver. Hepatic re-esterification and VLDL production can exceed oxidation/export capacity, causing steatosis.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1214 (Liver Metabolism and Fatty Liver, written source p. 135, Explain 4): Explain why deficiency of methyl donors can cause fatty liver.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 135, Explain 4
**EXP:** Choline/methionine-dependent methylation supports phosphatidylcholine formation, which is needed to assemble and export VLDL. Deficiency impairs triacylglycerol export, so fat accumulates in hepatocytes.
**Source Pages:** 135
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1215 (Blood Buffers and Acid-Base Imbalance, written source p. 138, Explain 1): Explain how the lungs and kidneys compensate for metabolic acidosis.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 138, Explain 1
**EXP:** The lungs rapidly increase ventilation, lowering PCO₂ and partially raising pH. Over hours to days, kidneys increase H⁺ excretion as titratable acid and NH₄⁺, reclaim filtered bicarbonate, and generate new bicarbonate.
**Source Pages:** 138
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---

### Q1216 (Blood Buffers and Acid-Base Imbalance, written source p. 138, Explain 2): Explain why the bicarbonate system is the most important extracellular buffer system.

**Correct Answer:** -
**Answer Source:** derived
**Source Question:** p. 138, Explain 2
**EXP:** The bicarbonate/CO₂ pair is abundant and functions as an open system: lungs regulate CO₂ rapidly and kidneys regulate bicarbonate and net acid excretion. This coordinated control makes it especially effective in extracellular fluid despite bicarbonate’s pKa being below physiologic pH.
**Source Pages:** 138
**Year:** None
**Tag:** Department, QBank, Biochemistry
**tagSuggere:** Biochemistry

---
